Geometric universal Jones invariant from configurations on ovals in the disc
Cristina Anghel, / ⁣ / /\!\!/, 9 th May 2025

Abstract
We construct geometrically a universal Jones invariant as a limit of invariants given by graded intersections in configuration spaces. For any fixed level 𝒩 \mathcal{N} , we construct a new knot invariant, called “ 𝒩 t ​ h \mathcal{N}^{th} Unified Jones invariant” globalising topologically all coloured Jones polynomials at levels less than 𝒩 \mathcal{N} . It is defined via the set of intersection points between Lagrangian submanifolds supported on arcs and ovals in the disc. Habiro’s famous invariant for knots [ 17 ] is a universal invariant recovering each coloured Jones polynomial. Willetts [ 31 ] modified Habiro’s ring and defined a universal invariant recovering coloured Jones polynomials and the ADO invariant divided by the Alexander polynomials. The universal Jones invariant that we construct belongs to a different ring that comes with a map to Habiro-Willetts’s ring. We prove that our invariant recovers Habiro-Willetts’s invariant. The difference is that our invariant is constructed as a limit of new knot invariants, the 𝒩 t ​ h \mathcal{N}^{th} unified Jones invariants. These invariants in turn provide a geometrical understanding of sets of all coloured Jones polynomials of bounded colour, collecting more information as we increase the colour. This article provides part of the set-up for a sequel, where we construct geometrically two universal link invariants . These invariants, universal Jones link invariant and universal ADO link invariant, globalise all coloured Jones polynomials and all ADO link invariants respectively. They are constructed as limits of link invariants that see all coloured Jones link invariants and coloured Alexander link invariants with bounded (multi) colours respectively. This answers the open problem of defining universal non-semisimple link invariants.

0
footnotetext:
Key words and phrases
: Quantum invariants, Topological models, Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev invariants.

1
Introduction

Coloured Jones and coloured Alexander polynomials are quantum link invariants coming from representation theory (
[
18
]
,
[
24
]
,
[
32
]
). The geometry and topology encoded by these invariants is an important open problem in quantum topology. Physics predicts that their asymptotics encode rich geometrical information of knot complements, such as the Volume Conjecture (Kashaev
[
25
]
,
[
29
]
). There are also important questions about globalisations when the colour tends to infinity. In
[
17
]
Habiro defined a powerful universal invariant that unifies algebraically all coloured Jones polynomials for knots.

Problem:

Unification of all coloured Jones polynomials of a bounded level

For a fixed level
\mathcal{N}
, can one construct an invariant that recovers all coloured Jones polynomials of colour bounded by
\mathcal{N}
? Can we do such a construction in a purely topological manner?

Our main result is an answer to this problem.

Theorem 1.1

(
New level
\mathcal{N}
invariants)

For each
\mathcal{N}
, we construct geometrically an invariant
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)
via Lagrangian intersections in a fixed configuration space in the disc. We call
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N}}(K)

\mathcal{N}^{th}
unified Jones invariant
and prove that it unifies all coloured Jones polynomials up to level
\mathcal{N}
:

(1.1)

\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)|_{\hat{\psi}^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{M}}}=J_{\mathcal{M}}(K),\forall\mathcal{M}\leqslant\mathcal{N}.

Theorem 1.2

(
Universal geometrical Jones invariant)

We define a knot invariant
{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)
taking values in a completed ring
\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}
that recovers all coloured Jones polynomials.
This invariant
{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)
is a limit of the unified Jones invariants defined in configuration spaces:

(1.2)

{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K):=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}

The geometrical origin of this universal invariant provides a new topological perspective for the asymptotics of these invariants, for which a purely topological
3
-dimensional description is a deep problem in quantum topology.
The construction of the universal invariant is done in three main steps, as below.

1.

We provide the
first topological model
for
coloured Alexander polynomials and coloured Jones polynomials for coloured links
(Theorem
2.6
)

2.

Then we construct new geometric knot invariants at level
\mathcal{N}
:

\mathcal{N}^{th}
unified Jones invariant
(Theorem
2.11
)

3.

Then we obtain the
Geometric Universal invariant from configurations on ovals:

Universal geometrical Jones invariant
(Theorem
5.5
).

Construction
The geometrical part of the construction is done in three main steps. First, for a fixed level
\mathcal{N}
and a braid
\beta_{n}
, we define a state sum of Lagrangian intersections

\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]

in the configuration space of
(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)+2
points in the punctured disc. This is given by the set of Lagrangian intersections
\{\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\rangle\}
from Figure
1.3
. We prove the following.

Theorem 1.3

(
Interpolating all coloured Jones polynomials of bounded level and the
\mathcal{N}^{th}
ADO invariant)

For any fixed level
\mathcal{N}\in\mathbb{N}
and
K=\hat{\beta_{n}}
a knot seen as a closure of a braid
\beta_{n}
, the graded intersection
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})
recovers all coloured Jones polynomials up to level
\mathcal{N}
and also the
\mathcal{N}^{th}
coloured Alexander polynomial via specialisation of coefficients (as in Figure
1.1
).

Then, we define a sequence of nested ideals
\cdots\supseteq\tilde{I}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\supseteq\tilde{I}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}+1}\supseteq\cdots
in
\mathbb{L}
, given by the formulas:

(1.3)

\tilde{I}_{\mathcal{N}}^{J}:=\langle\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}(xd^{i-1}-1)\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{L}.\\

We obtain a sequence of quotient rings, with maps between them and their projective limit
\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}
:

(1.4)

\displaystyle l^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\hskip 31.29802ptl^{J}_{\mathcal{N}+1}

\displaystyle\cdots\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\longleftarrow\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}+1}\longleftarrow\cdots\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}:=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}.

The
\mathcal{N}^{th}
Unified Jones invariant,
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
is the image of the intersection
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})
in the
\mathcal{N}^{th}
quotient ring
\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
. It is a well-defined knot invariant recovering all coloured Jones polynomials up to level
\mathcal{N}
(see Theorem
2.11
).
Then, we prove that we can pass to the limit, and define the universal geometrical Jones invariant
{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}
:

(1.5)

{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K):=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K).

The relations between the above invariants through the lenses of the common geometric construction are summarised in Figure
1.1
.

\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\ni\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)
J_{\mathcal{N}}(K)\ \ \ \cdots
J_{\mathcal{N}-1}(K)
J_{2}(K)
{\Phi^{\mathcal{N}}}(K)\ \ \ \cdots
\Phi^{\mathcal{N}-1}(K)
{\Phi^{2}}(K)
\mathcal{N}^{th}
unified Jones inv.

Lagrangian intersection

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Conf}_{2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}\left(\mathbb{D}\right)}

\ \ \ \ \ \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{L}

Universal Coloured Jones inv.

{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}
Coloured Jones polynomials
with colors bounded by
\mathcal{N}
Coloured Jones polynomials
\mathcal{N}^{th}
Coloured Alexander polynomial
\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}

Figure 1.1
:
Geometrical universal Jones invariant for knots

1.1
Historical motivation and Results

Coloured Jones polynomials come from the quantum group
U_{q}(sl(2))
. To each
l
-component link
L
and a choice of colours
N_{1},...,N_{l}\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
they associate a polynomial
J_{N_{1},...,N_{l}}(L,q)
. Dually, for a level
\mathcal{N}\in\mathbb{N}
(
\mathcal{N}\geqslant 2
), the quantum group at the root of unity
\xi_{N}=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{2\mathcal{N}}}
gives non-semisimple quantum link invariants. They associate to a link
L
coloured with generic colours
\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{l}\in\mathbb{C}
an invariant
\Phi^{\mathcal{N}}_{\bar{\lambda}}(L)\in\mathbb{C}
, called coloured Alexander polynomial
[
1
]
(here
\bar{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{l})
). As we have seen, there are important questions about the asymptotics of these invariants. Habiro’s celebrated invariant is a power series that unifies and recovers all coloured Jones polynomials of a knot. Also, the Melvin-Morton-Rozansky Conjecture (MMR
[
28
]
, proved in
[
10
]
) shows that this knot invariant contains powers of the Alexander polynomial, as denominators. A powerful result due to Willetts
[
31
]
showed that for knots the above loop expansion recovers the ADO invariants divided by Alexander polynomials. The geometric meaning of the coefficients of this expansion is the subject of many interesting conjectures. For instance, Gukov and Manolescu (
[
14
]
) introduced a geometric construction which leads to a power series from knot complements and conjectured that it recovers the loop expansion.

The story for coloured link invariants is completely different. As we have seen, Habiro-Willetts’s model provides universal invariants unifying knot invariants. Until now, there are no universal invariants defined for the link case. On the other hand, such coloured versions of quantum link invariants are building blocks for
3-
manifold invariants. Coloured Jones polynomials of a link with colours less than
\mathcal{N}
give the Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev invariant of the surgery along the link. Dually, the ADO polynomials of a link with bounded colours give the Costantino-Geer-Patureau invariant. These are powerful invariants which detect lens spaces. A
3
-dimensional description and categorifications for the
WRT
(and
CGP
) invariants are major problems in quantum topology.

Our aim is to study quantum invariants from a new topological perspective: as intersection pairings between homology classes in coverings of configuration spaces. We call such a description a “topological model”. Topological models for knot invariants appeared in
[
11
]
,
[
26
]
,
[
3
]
,
[
4
]
,
[
5
]
.

In
[
5
]
, we provided a topological model for coloured Jones polynomials via configurations on
figure eights
in the disc. Then, in
[
6
]
we proved the
first topological model
for
3
-manifold invariants, for Witten-Reshetikhin-Turaev’s invariant. We showed that the
\mathcal{N}^{th}
WRT can be read off from a set of Lagrangians in a fixed configuration space, providing a new framework for the study of these invariants for which a complete
3
-dimensional topological description and categorifications are open questions. As a parallel, there are no topological models known for the Costantino-Geer-Patureau invariants. Motivated by Habiro’s unifications and problems regarding the topology of non-semisimple
3
-manifold invariants we pose four questions.

•

Question 1
Construct
topological models
for coloured Jones and coloured Alexander invariants for
coloured links rather than for knots
.

Up to this moment no such model was known for the coloured Alexander invariants.

•

Question 2
Having in mind Floer type categorifications, can we provide topological models with
embedded topological supports
, given by

ovals rather than figure eights
?

•

Question 3
Is it possible to construct

universal invariants for links rather than for knots
? In the sequel
[
8
]
we answer this open question,

constructing universal invariants for links
.

•

Question 4
Are there

topological models for CGP invariants
? (For the WRT invariants, we provided a topological model with figure eights in
[
6
]
)

In this paper we answer the first two questions from above. The answer to
Question 2
provides a new perspective and makes possible the definition of our geometrical universal invariant:

•

in Theorem
2.8
and Theorem
2.6
we show that coloured Jones and coloured Alexander invariants for links can be read off from the same set of Lagrangian intersections in a fixed configuration space, based on arcs and ovals,

•

Theorem
2.9
provides topological models for coloured Jones invariants that behave well with respect to change of colours, and leads to the universal Jones invariant from Theorem
5.5
.

The response for
Question 1
gives the first topological models for non-semisimple invariants for links. This is the building block for two sequel papers that answer the remaining questions. In the first sequel article we answer
Question 3
providing universal invariants for links. Also, another sequel paper answers
Question 4
on topological models for non-semisimple
3
-manifold invariants.

1.2
Recovering Habiro-Willetts’s invariant

In this part we analyse similarities and differences between our invariant and the Habiro-Willetts universal invariant.
In
[
17
]
, Habiro defined a two-variable power series constructed in his Universal Ring for knots, which unifies all coloured Jones polynomials, and conjectured that at roots of unity this recovers coloured Alexander polynomials over the Alexander polynomials.
In
[
31
]
, Willetts changed the description of the ring introduced by Habiro by considering another sequence of ideals and associated quotient rings
\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}_{\mathcal{N}}
, as in relation (
1.10
). They give in the limit a universal ring
\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}:=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}_{\mathcal{N}}.
Habiro-Willetts’s Universal invariant
{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{W}}(K)
belongs to
\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}
and it comes from Lawrence’s universal construction. This invariant recovers coloured Jones polynomials through generic specialisations of coefficients and the ADO invariants divided by the Alexander polynomial through specialisations at roots of unity (as stated in Theorem
10.4
). However, this invariant is constructed directly in the limit ring:
{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{W}}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}
.
We show that our universal invariant recovers Habiro-Willetts’s invariant (Theorem
10.5
).

Theorem 1.4

(
Projection onto Habiro-Willetts’s Universal invariant
)

There is a natural map between the universal rings
\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}
and
\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}
which sends our universal Jones invariant to Habiro-Willetts’s universal invariant:

\pi:\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}\rightarrow\ \hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}

(1.6)

\pi({\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K))={\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{W}}(K).

As a consequence, we obtain the following.

Theorem 1.5

(
Geometric unification of coloured Jones and Alexander invariants
[
31
]
)

The universal geometrical Jones invariant that we construct recovers coloured Jones polynomials and the ADO invariants divided by the Alexander polynomial:

(1.7)

\displaystyle\large{\displaystyle\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)\Bigm|_{d=\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{-1}}=~\frac{\Phi^{\mathcal{N}}(K,x)}{\Delta(K,x^{2\mathcal{N}})}

\displaystyle\large{\displaystyle\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)\Bigm|_{x=d^{\mathcal{N}-1}}=~J_{\mathcal{N}}(K).

1.3
Differences: the geometrical Jones invariant and Habiro-Willetts’s invariant

In this part, we investigate the components of the two universal invariants that we have:

{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K):=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}\ \ \ \longrightarrow\ \ \ {\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{W}}(K):=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},W}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}

Structurally, these two universal invariants come from totally different perspectives. The geometric universal Jones invariant is a limit of invariants that see more and more coloured Jones polynomials:

(1.8)

\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)\Bigm|_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}x=d^{\mathcal{M}-1}}=~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}J_{\mathcal{M}}(K)},\ \ \forall\mathcal{M}\leqslant\mathcal{N}.

On the other hand, the structure of the Habiro-Willetts invariant is different, being naturally constructed in the limit ring. In Section
10
, we discuss the structure of Willetts’s ring and we investigate its components. Since the ideals that he considered are much larger than ours, its intermediary rings are smaller. Because of this, the specialisations at natural numbers or roots of unity are impossible from the
\mathcal{N}^{th}
component of Habiro-Willetts’s invariant:

(1.9)

\displaystyle\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},W}(K)\Bigm|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{x=d^{\mathcal{M}-1}}}\text{not well-defined},

\displaystyle\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},W}(K)\Bigm|_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{d=\xi_{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}}}\text{ not well-defined},\ \ \ \ \ \forall\mathcal{M}\leqslant\mathcal{N}.

Remark 1.6

(Unifying intermediary levels of the coloured Jones sequence)

So the question of having a knot invariant at Willetts’s intermediary levels that sees either coloured Jones invariants or coloured Alexander invariants is impossible even to state. On the other hand, in our ring, which is larger, we are able to globalise more and more coloured Jones polynomials and in the limit we recover also the ADO invariant over the Alexander polynomial, as summarised in Figure
1.2
.

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}=\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]/\langle\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}(xd^{i-1}-1)\rangle.

\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\ni\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)
\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}_{\mathcal{N}}
J_{\mathcal{N}}(K)
J_{\mathcal{N}-1}(K)
J_{\mathcal{M}}(K)
\frac{\Phi^{\mathcal{N}}(K)}{\Delta(x^{2\mathcal{N}})}
\frac{\Phi^{\mathcal{N}-1}(K)}{\Delta(x^{2(\mathcal{N}-1})}
\frac{\Phi^{\mathcal{M}}(K)}{\Delta(x^{2\mathcal{M}})}
\mathcal{N}^{th}
unified Jones inv.
Universal Geometrical Jones invariant
Habiro-Willetts universal invariant
{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}
{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{W}}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}
\nexists
\pi
\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}

(1.10)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}_{\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{W}_{\mathcal{N}}=\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]/\langle\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}(xd^{i-1}-1)\prod_{i=k-1}^{\mathcal{N}-1}(d^{i}-1)\mid 1\leqslant k\leqslant\mathcal{N}-1\rangle.

:
The universal Jones invariant globalises sequences of coloured Jones invariants

1.4
Sequel paper I: universal geometrical link invariants

This article is paired with a sequel paper, concerning the following problem.

Open problem:

Can one construct a unification of coloured Alexander invariants (which are non-semisimple) for coloured links? Our second paper provides an answer to this open problem.

The parallel question about semi-simple invariants for knots is the subject of Habiro’s famous universal knot invariant, for which we presented a geometric perspective in the current paper.

In the sequel paper, we construct geometrically two
universal link invariants
, as limits of invariants defined in configuration spaces.
More specifically, we define a universal ADO link invariant as a limit of invariants given by graded intersections in configuration spaces. Moreover, we construct a universal Jones invariant for links, which is a limit of invariants globalising all coloured Jones link invariants at bounded levels.

This models uses in an essential manner the geometrical set-up from the current paper: the topological model for coloured Alexander link invariants from Theorem
2.6
. However, it contains new geometric data which permits globalisations for the link case.

1.5
Sequel paper II: Topological models for
WRT
and
CGP
invariants

An active research area studies categorifications of link invariants (
[
25
,
23
,
22
,
30
,
27
]
). As discussed, the story is different for the analogous
3
-manifold invariants. The existence of categorifications for
WRT
and
CGP
invariants are open questions.
For this purpose, a topological model with embedded Lagrangians would bring a new approach to these problems.
In a different direction, passing from knot to
3
-manifold invariants, Habiro
[
17
]
showed a beautiful unification of all
WRT
invariants.

Problem-
Unification for non-semisimple
3
-manifold invariants

Is it possible to unify the
CGP
invariants, as a parallel to the celebrated unification of the WRT invariants, proved by Habiro (
[
17
]
)?

As a step towards this question, we shall prove the following results in a sequel paper.

Using the models from this paper for coloured Jones and Alexander invariants for links, we pass to
3
-manifolds. Our sequel result describes both
WRT
and
CGP
invariants from a set of Lagrangian submanifolds in a fixed configuration space. The geometric support of these Lagrangians will be based on ovals, which is important for the geometric study of these invariants.

1.6
Geometry of the universal Jones invariant via infinite configuration spaces

A

As we have seen, the universal Jones invariant
{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)
is defined as a limit of the
\mathcal{N}^{th}
unified Jones invariants
\{\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\}
.
For a fixed colour
\mathcal{N}
,
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)
is given by graded intersections in the configuration space of
(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)+2
points in the disc :

\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)\longleftrightarrow\{(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\},\text{ for }{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}}.

A

Figure 1.3
:
Universal Jones invariant as the limit of intersections in configurations

Passing from level
\mathcal{N}
to level
\mathcal{N}+1
, we have an explicitly understandable behaviour of the homology classes, as shown in Figure
1.3
.
The above argument suggests that the behaviour of the knot invariants
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N}}(K)
with respect to the change of level has a stability phenomenon.
Based on this we are interested in defining the universal Jones invariant
{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)
directly as an intersection in an infinite configuration space.

Structure of the paper

This paper has seven main parts. In Section
4
we define homological tools: local systems and homology groups. Then, in Section
4.9
we show that for good choices of covering spaces, we can lift submanifolds supported on ovals. Section
5
has the topological model for coloured Jones polynomials. Section
6
is dedicated to the topological model for coloured Alexander polynomials for links. Then, in Section
7
we show the globalisation property of our topological model for coloured Jones polynomials at bounded level. Based on this, we define our unified Jones invariants in Section
8
. In Section
9.1
we construct the universal Jones invariant
{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)
, a knot invariant recovering all coloured Jones polynomials. In Section
10
we show that our invariant recovers Habiro-Willets’s invariant by a change of coefficients and discuss the relation between these two universal constructions.

2
Summary of the two topological models

2.1
Geometrical set-up

Suppose that
L
link, and let
\beta_{n}\in B_{n}
be a braid such that
L=\widehat{\beta_{n}}
by braid closure. Let
\mathbb{D}_{2n+2}
be the
(2n+2)
-punctured disc where we split the set of punctures:

•

2n
punctures placed horizontally, called
p
-punctures (denoted by
\{1,..,2n\}
)

•

1
puncture called
q
-puncture (labeled by
\{0\}
)

•

1
puncture placed as in Figure
1.3
(below the last
p
-puncture) called
s
-puncture.

Now, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let
C_{n,m}:=\mathrm{Conf}_{m}(\mathbb{D}_{2n+2})
be the unordered configuration space of
m
points in the punctured disc. A subtle part of our construction is the definition of a suitable covering space.
Suppose that we fix
\bar{\mathcal{N}}=(\mathcal{N}_{1},\mathcal{N}_{2},...,\mathcal{N}_{n})
, called “(multi-)level”, with
\mathcal{N}_{1}+\mathcal{N}_{2}+...+\mathcal{N}_{n}=m-1
.

Difficulty
Let
L(\bar{\mathcal{N}})\subseteq\mathrm{Conf}_{m}(\mathbb{D}_{2n+2})
be the submanifold given by
configurations on symmetric ovals
with multiplicities prescribed by
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
, as in Figure
4.3
. We want to construct a covering of
C_{n,m}
where
L(\bar{\mathcal{N}})
has a well-defined lift. In the literature there are two well known local systems given by the Lawrence representation
[
26
]
obtained by evaluating symmetric punctures by the same or opposite variables. Our
L(\bar{\mathcal{N}})
does
not
have a lift in the two associated
Lawrence covering spaces
.

Definition 2.1

(
Level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
covering space)

In order to be able to have
well-defined lifts
of
L(\bar{\mathcal{N}})
, we construct a well-chosen covering that depends on the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
. We do so by defining a finer local system
\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
on
\mathrm{Conf}_{m}(\mathbb{D}_{2n+2})
(Definition
4.8
), associated to the
level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
. This evaluates monodromies around symmetric punctures
i
and
2n-i
by different variables, depending on the multiplicity on the
i^{th}
oval:
\mathcal{N}_{i}
. Next, we use the homologies of the associated covering space:
H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,m}
and
H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,m}
(they are versions of Borel-Moore homology introduced in
[
9
]
, see Diagram
4.2
).

Definition 2.2

(Intersection pairing)

There is a Poincaré-type duality relating these homologies:

\left\langle~,~\right\rangle:H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,m}\otimes H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\ \ (\text{Proposition }\ref{P:3'})
and moreover there is a braid group action on
H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,m}
(as in Proposition
4.36
).

Remark 2.3

(
New framework: Topological models from configurations on ovals)

The submanifold
L(\bar{\mathcal{N}})
has a well-defined lift in the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
covering, and gives a well defined homology class. This lifting result is definitely false if we take the local system that naturally arises from the Lawrence representation, and do not quotient further using the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
.
This is the reason that prevented us from having a model with configurations on ovals for coloured Jones polynomials and coloured Alexander polynomials in
[
5
]
and for WRT invariants in
[
6
]
.

2.2
First Topological model for coloured Alexander polynomials for links

Let us fix a level
\mathcal{N}
and
L
an oriented framed link with framings
f_{1},...,f_{l}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\beta_{n}\in B_{n}
such that
L=\widehat{\beta_{n}}
. For the coloured Alexander polynomial
\Phi^{\mathcal{N}}_{\bar{\lambda}}
we use the above homological set-up associated to the data:

•

Number of particles
m_{A}(\mathcal{N}):=2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)
, Space
C_{n,m_{A}(\mathcal{N})}:=\mathrm{Conf}_{2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}\left(\mathbb{D}_{2n+2}\right)

•

Multi-level:
\bar{\mathcal{N}}:=(\mathcal{N}_{1},...,\mathcal{N}_{n})=(1,\mathcal{N}-1,...,\mathcal{N}-1)
; Local system:
\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}

•

Specialisation of coefficients:
\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}
(Notation
3.15
, Definition
3.16
).

•

Indexing set:
\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}=\big\{\bar{i}=(i_{1},...,i_{n-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}\mid 0\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant\mathcal{N}-1,\ \forall 1\leqslant k\leqslant n-1\big\}.

Definition 2.4

(Coloured Homology classes) We use the homology classes of lifts of Lagrangian submanifolds from the base space. They are prescribed by collection of arcs/ ovals in the disc as in Notation
4.31
.
With this recipe, for
\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}
we define two classes:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,m_{A}(\mathcal{N})}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,m_{A}(\mathcal{N})}}.

Figure 2.1
:
Coloured Alexander Homology Classes

We look at the link as being the closure of a braid with
n
strands. This induces a colouring
C
of
2n
points with
l
colours, which we denote by:

(2.1)

C:\{1,...,2n\}\rightarrow\{1,...,l\}.

Definition 2.5

(
Lagrangian intersection for coloured Alexander polynomials)

We define the following Lagrangian intersection in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathrm{Conf}_{2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}\left(\mathbb{D}_{2n+2}\right)}
:

(2.2)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{Z}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},...,u_{l}^{\pm 1},x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{l}u_{i}^{\left(f_{i}-\sum_{j\neq{i}}lk_{i,j}\right)}\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n}u^{-1}_{C(i)}\cdot\sum_{\bar{i}=\bar{0}}^{\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle.

Theorem 2.6

(
Topological model for coloured Alexander polynomials of coloured links)

The graded intersection
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})
recovers the
\mathcal{N}^{th}
coloured Alexander polynomial of
L
as below:

(2.3)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathcal{N}}_{\bar{\lambda}}(L)=~\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\Bigm|_{\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}}\ \ \ (\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}\text{ is a specialisation of variables}).

This is the
first topological model for these non-semisimple link invariants
coloured with different colours. For uni-coloured links we constructed topological models in
[
3
,
5
]
. From the representation theory perspective, the change from the
single to multi-colour case
is subtle due to the fact that the non-semisimple quantum invariants for coloured links strongly rely on the notion of modified traces and modified quantum dimensions.

2.2.1
Encoding modified quantum dimensions via topological tools

We encode the modified dimensions using punctures and local systems on configuration spaces. Our idea is to add an additional puncture, the
s
-puncture
which
encodes modified quantum dimensions
. We do this via the local system, by recording the monodromy around this puncture by a variable
y
. Then, we define our graded intersection
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})
which also has a contribution in
y
. At the very end, we specialise
y
to the modified dimension through the function
\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}
.

Remark 2.7

(
Role of the variables
) The pairing
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})
has
4
types of variables:
x_{i},y,d,u_{i}
. The first three variables come from the geometry of the local system, while the last one,
u_{i}
, encodes algebraic data provided by the pivotal structure coming from the quantum group.

•

[

Winding around the link
] Variables
x_{i}
encode
linking numbers with the link
, as winding numbers around the
p
-punctures

•

[

Relative twisting
] The power of
d
records a relative twisting of the submanifolds, given by the winding around the
diagonal of the symmetric power
.

•

[

Modified dimension
] The power of
y
of records the winding number around the

s
-puncture

•

[

Pivotal structure
]

u_{i}
captures the
semisimplicity/ non-semisimplicity
of the invariant, which is given by a power of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{i}}
, so a
power of the linking number with the link
.

2.3
Topological model- coloured Jones polynomials

Let
N_{1},...,N_{l}\in\mathbb{N}
be a set of colours for our link and
\bar{N}:=(N_{1},...,N_{l}).
Using the colouring
C
defined above, we denote
N^{C}_{i}:=N_{C(i)}.

Parameters for
J_{N_{1},...,N_{l}}
This time, we use the homological set-up associated to the data:

•

Number of particles:
m_{J}(\bar{N}):=2+\sum_{i=2}^{n}N^{C}_{i}

•

Configuration space:
C_{n,m_{J}(\bar{N})}:=\mathrm{Conf}_{2+\sum_{i=2}^{n}N^{C}_{i}}\left(\mathbb{D}_{2n+2}\right)

•

Multi-level:
\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N}):=(1,N^{C}_{2}-1,...,N^{C}_{n}-1)
, Local system:
\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N})}
(
depend on the colours
)

•

Specialisation of coefficients:
\psi_{\bar{N}}
(Notation
3.12
, Definition
3.13
).

We remark that the number of particles
m_{J}(\bar{N})
, the multi-level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N})
and local system
\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N})}
depend on
\bar{N}
. So, the good covering in this case depends on the colouring. Now, let us define:

(2.4)

C(\bar{N}):=\big\{\bar{i}=(i_{1},...,i_{n-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}\mid 0\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant N^{C}_{k+1}-1,\ \forall k\in\{1,...,n-1\}\big\}.

For
\bar{i}\in C(\bar{N})
consider the two homology classes given by the geometric supports from Figure
2.2
:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N})}_{n,m_{J}(\bar{N})}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N}),\partial}_{n,m_{J}(\bar{N})}}.

Figure 2.2
:

Lagrangians for Coloured Jones link invariants coloured with diferent colours

Theorem 2.8

(
Topological model for coloured Jones polynomials for coloured links)

Let us consider the Lagrangian intersection
J{\Gamma}^{\bar{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{L}
, given by:

(2.5)

J{\Gamma}^{\bar{N}}(\beta_{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{l}u_{i}^{\left(f_{i}-\sum_{j\neq{i}}lk_{i,j}\right)}\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n}x^{-1}_{C(i)}\cdot\sum_{\bar{i}\in C(\bar{N})}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle.

Then
J{\Gamma}^{\bar{N}}(\beta_{n})
recovers the coloured Jones polynomial of
L
through specialisations:

(2.6)

J_{N_{1},...,N_{l}}(L,q)=J{\Gamma}^{\bar{N}}(\beta_{n})\Bigm|_{\psi_{\bar{N}}}.

2.3.1
Knot closures

From now on we will be looking at the case of knots
K
, seen as closure of braids
\beta_{n}
, in the context described above (for
l=1
). In this situation, the intersection pairing for coloured Jones polynomials and coloured Alexander polynomials with colour
\mathcal{N}
coincide:

(2.7)

\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})=J\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}[u^{\pm 1},x^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}].

From Theorem
2.8
and Theorem
2.6
we know that this intersection recovers the two invariants at level
\mathcal{N}
, as below:

(2.8)

\displaystyle J_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}}(K)=\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\Bigm|_{\psi_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}}}

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathcal{N}}_{\bar{\lambda}}(K)=\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\Bigm|_{\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}}.

Theorem 2.9

(
Unifying all coloured Jones polynomials of bounded level)

Let us fix
\mathcal{N}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})
recovers all coloured Jones polynomials of colours
\mathcal{M}\leqslant\mathcal{N}
, as below:

(2.9)

\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{M}}(K)

\displaystyle=~\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\Bigm|_{\psi_{\bar{\mathcal{M}}}}.

2.3.2
New invariants at level
\mathcal{N}

Convention 2.10

Let us fix the ring
\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]
. For the following part, having in mind that we work with knots, for the simplicity of the construction we set
y=1
. If we do so, our intersection form
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})
recovers the normalised versions of the
\mathcal{N}^{th}
coloured Jones and Alexander polynomials. We denote by
J_{\mathcal{N}}
the
{\mathcal{N}}^{th}
normalised coloured Jones polynomial (in the above section we had
J_{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
the un-normalised version of this invariant).

Further, by setting
u=x
we can look at the intersection

\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]

and all the specialisations associated to the coloured Jones polynomials induce well-defined specialisations from
\mathbb{L}
, which we denote by
\psi^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
, as in formula (
3.17
):

(2.10)

\psi^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}(x)=d^{1-\mathcal{N}}.

Now for a fixed level
\mathcal{N}
, we have defined our state sum of Lagrangian intersections
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{L}
in the
configuration space of
(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)+2
points in the disc. This is given by the set of Lagrangian intersections
\{\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\rangle\}_{\bar{i}\in C(\mathcal{N})}.
For the next step, we define a sequence of nested ideals in
\mathbb{L}
, denoted by
\cdots\supseteq\tilde{I}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\supseteq\tilde{I}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}+1}\supseteq\cdots
, given by the formulas:

(2.11)

\tilde{I}_{\mathcal{N}}^{J}:=\langle\prod_{i=1}^{\mathcal{N}}(xd^{i-1}-1)\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{L}.\\

In this manner, we obtain a sequence of associated quotient rings, with maps between them:

(2.12)

\displaystyle l^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\hskip 31.29802ptl^{J}_{\mathcal{N}+1}

\displaystyle\cdots\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\longleftarrow\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}+1}\longleftarrow\cdots\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}.

In Theorem
8.13
we prove the following.

Theorem 2.11

(
\mathcal{N}^{th}
Unified Jones invariant)

Let
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)
be the image of the intersection
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})
in the
\mathcal{N}^{th}
quotient ring
\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
. Then
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)
is a well-defined knot invariant recovering all coloured Jones polynomials up to level
\mathcal{N}
:

\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)|_{\hat{\psi}^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{M}}}=J_{\mathcal{M}}(K),\ \ \ \ \ \forall\mathcal{M}\leqslant\mathcal{N}.

Here,
\hat{\psi}^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{M}}
is the change of coefficients from relation (
8.9
). We call
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)
the
\mathcal{N}^{th}
Unified Jones invariant.

2.4
Construction of the Geometric Universal Invariant

In this part we show that the level
\mathcal{N}
unified Jones invariants have a good asymptotic behaviour and they give knot invariants in the associated projective limit ring. The key result that makes this unification possible is Theorem
2.9
.

Definition 2.12

(Limit ring)

We denote the projective limit of this sequence of rings as follows:

(2.13)

\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}:=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}.

Theorem 2.13

(
Universal Coloured Jones Invariant)

We construct a well-defined knot invariant
{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}
as a limit of the graded intersections via configuration spaces on ovals, recovering all coloured Jones polynomials:

(2.14)

J_{\mathcal{N}}(K)={\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)|_{\psi^{u,J}_{\mathcal{N}}}.

In this expression
\psi^{u,J}_{\mathcal{N}}
is a ring homomorphism (see Definition
3.19
).

Definition 2.14

\bar{\lambda}

3
Notations

Notation 3.1

(Specialisations of modules)

Let
N
be a module over a ring
R
. Let
R^{\prime}
be another ring and suppose that we have a specialisation of the coefficients, meaning a morphism:

\psi:R\rightarrow R^{\prime}.

We denote by

N|_{\psi}:=N\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}

the specialisation of the module
N
by the function
\psi
.

Notation 3.2

(Quantum numbers and modified dimensions)

\{x\}_{q}:=q^{x}-q^{-x}\ \ \ \ [x]_{q}:=\frac{q^{x}-q^{-x}}{q-q^{-1}}.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\xi_{N}=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{2N}}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, the associated modified dimension is given by:

d(\lambda):=\frac{\{\lambda\}_{\xi_{N}}}{\{N\lambda\}_{\xi_{N}}}.

Definition 3.3

(Left
/\penalty
right hand side of the disc) We separate the punctured disc into two halves, as follows:

•

(Left hand side of the punctured disc) This will be the half of the disc from Figure
2.3
that contains the first
n

p
-punctures and passes though the puncture labeled by
0
.

•

(Right hand side of the punctured disc) This will be the other half of the disc from Figure
2.3
that contains the rest of the
p
-punctures.

Definition 3.4

(Multi-indices at level
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
and
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
)

We will use the following sets of multi-indices, which will parametrise the states for our models:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}:=\big\{\bar{i}=(i_{1},...,i_{n-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}\mid 0\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant\mathcal{N}-1,\ \forall k\in\{1,...,n-1\}\big\}

\displaystyle\{\bar{\mathcal{M}},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}:=\big\{\bar{i}=(i_{1},...,i_{n-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}\mid 0\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant\mathcal{N}-1,\ \forall k\in\{1,...,n-1\}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\exists j\in\{1,...,n-1\},\ \mathcal{M}\leqslant i_{j}\big\}

Definition 3.5

(Multi-indices)

For a choice of parameters
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
l\geqslant 1
and
\alpha_{1},..,\alpha_{l},\eta,..,\eta_{l}\in\mathbb{C}
,
N_{1},...,N_{l}\in\mathbb{N}
we denote the following associated multi-indices:

(3.2)

\begin{cases}\bar{\alpha}:=(\alpha_{1},..,\alpha_{l})\\
\bar{\eta}:=(\eta_{1},..,\eta_{l})\\
\bar{N}:=(N_{1},...,N_{l}).\end{cases}

3.1
Specialisations for the homology groups- first specialisation and globalised specialisation

Definition 3.6

(Specialisations for the homology groups)

Let us consider two multi-indices:
\bar{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}^{l}
and
\bar{\eta}\in(\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}])^{\bar{l}}
. We denote the following specialisation of coefficients:

\psi_{q,\bar{\alpha},\bar{\eta}}:\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1},...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},q^{\pm\frac{\alpha_{1}}{2}},...,q^{\pm\frac{\alpha_{l}}{2}}]

(3.3)

\begin{cases}&\psi_{q,\bar{\alpha},\bar{\eta}}(x_{j})=q^{\alpha_{j}},\ j\in\{1,...,l\}\\
&\psi_{q,\bar{\alpha},\bar{\eta}}(y_{j})=\eta_{j},j\in\{1,...,l\}\\
&\psi_{q,\bar{\alpha},\bar{\eta}}(d)=q^{-1}.\end{cases}

Definition 3.7

(Globalised specialisations)

Let us consider an integer number
t
, and two multi-indices of
l
colours:
\bar{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}^{l}
and
\bar{\eta}\in\mathbb{C}^{l}
. Associated to this data, we define the specialisation of coefficients:

\psi^{t}_{q,\bar{\alpha},\bar{\eta}}:\mathbb{Z}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},...,u_{l}^{\pm 1},x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1},...,y_{l}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},q^{\pm\frac{\alpha_{1}}{2}},...,q^{\pm\frac{\alpha_{l}}{2}}]

(3.4)

\begin{cases}&\psi^{t}_{q,\bar{\alpha},\bar{\eta}}(u_{j})=\left(\psi^{t}_{q,\bar{\alpha},\bar{\eta}}(x_{j})\right)^{t}=q^{t\alpha_{j}}\\
&\psi^{t}_{q,\bar{\alpha},\bar{\eta}}(x_{j})=q^{\alpha_{j}},\ j\in\{1,...,l\}\\
&\psi^{t}_{q,\bar{\alpha},\bar{\eta}}(y_{j})=\eta_{j}\\
&\psi^{t}_{q,\bar{\alpha},\bar{\eta}}(d)=q^{-1}.\end{cases}

Definition 3.8

(Specialisations of coefficients)

For a colouring
C:\{1,...,n\}\rightarrow\{1,...,l\}
we consider the specialisation of coefficients as below:
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]
\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},q^{\pm\frac{\alpha_{1}}{2}},...,q^{\pm\frac{\alpha_{l}}{2}}]
f_{C}
(
4.19
)
\psi^{t}_{q,\bar{\alpha},\bar{\eta}}
(
3.4
)

Notation 3.9

In the formulas from the paper we denote by
N^{C}_{i}:=N_{C(i)}
.

Definition 3.10

(Our setting: specialisation corresponding to a braid closure)

We will use this change of coefficients in the situation where
n
is replaced by
2n
and these
2n
points inherit a colouring with
l
colours coming from a braid closure of a braid with
n
strands:

(3.5)

C:\{1,...,2n\}\rightarrow\{1,...,l\}.

3.2
Coloured Jones polynomials- generic parameters

Definition 3.11

(Specialisation for the classes associated to coloured Jones polynomials)

Let us fix the multi-indices:

(3.6)

\begin{cases}\bar{N}:=(N_{1}-1,...,N_{l}-1)\\
\overline{[N]}:=([N^{C}_{1}]_{q})\\
\end{cases}

Then, we obtain the following specialisation associated to
\bar{\alpha}=\bar{N}
and
\eta=\overline{[N]}
:

\psi_{q,\bar{N},\overline{[N]}}:\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]

(3.7)

\begin{cases}&\psi_{q,\bar{N},\overline{[N]}}(x_{i})=q^{N^{C}_{i}-1},\ i\in\{1,...,l\}\\
&\psi_{q,\bar{N},\overline{[N]}}(y)=[N^{C}_{1}]_{q},\\
&\psi_{q,\bar{N},\overline{[N]}}(d)=q^{-1}.\end{cases}

For the case of the link invariants, we will use the globalised specialisation associated to the multi-indices:

(3.8)

\begin{cases}\bar{N}:=(N_{1}-1,...,N_{l}-1)\\
\overline{[N]}:=([N^{C}_{1}]_{q})\\
t=1.\end{cases}

Notation 3.12

(Specialisation at generic
q
)

Let us denote the specialisation associated to the above parameters as:

(3.9)

\psi_{\bar{N}}:=\psi^{1}_{q,\bar{N},\overline{[N]}}.

Definition 3.13

(Specialisation for coloured Jones polynomials)

The associated specialisation of coefficients is given by:

\psi_{\bar{N}}:\mathbb{C}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},...,u_{l}^{\pm 1},x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]

(3.10)

\begin{cases}&\psi_{\bar{N}}(u_{j})=\left(\psi_{\bar{N}}(x_{j})\right)^{t}=q^{N_{i}-1}\\
&\psi_{\bar{N}}(x_{i})=q^{N_{i}-1},\ i\in\{1,...,l\}\\
&\psi_{\bar{N}}(y)=[N^{C}_{1}]_{q},\\
&\psi_{\bar{N}}(d)=q^{-1}.\end{cases}

3.3
Coloured Alexander polynomials- parameters at roots of unity

Definition 3.14

(Specialisation for the classes associated to coloured Alexander polynomials)

Let us fix the multi-indices:

(3.11)

\begin{cases}\bar{\lambda}:=(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{l})\\
\overline{d(\lambda)}:=([\lambda_{C(1)}]_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}})\\
\end{cases}

Then, we consider the following specialisation, which is associated to the multi-indices
\bar{\alpha}=\bar{\lambda}
and
\eta=\overline{d(\lambda)}
:

\psi_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}},\bar{\lambda},\overline{d(\lambda)}}:\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}},...,\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm\frac{\lambda_{l}}{2}}]

(3.12)

\begin{cases}&\psi_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}},\bar{\lambda},\overline{d(\lambda)}}(x_{i})=\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\lambda_{i}},\ i\in\{1,...,l\}\\
&\psi_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}},\bar{\lambda},\overline{d(\lambda)}}(y)=([\lambda_{C(1)}]_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}),\\
&\psi_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}},\bar{\lambda},\overline{d(\lambda)}}(d)=\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{-1}.\end{cases}

For this case of the link invariants, we use the globalised specialisation associated to the multi-indices:

(3.13)

\begin{cases}\bar{\lambda}:=(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{l})\\
\overline{d(\lambda)}:=([\lambda_{C(1)}]_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}})\\
t=1-\mathcal{N}.\end{cases}

Notation 3.15

(Specialisation at roots of unity)

Let us denote the specialisation associated to the above parameters as:

(3.14)

\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}:=\psi^{1-\mathcal{N}}_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}},\bar{\lambda},\overline{d(\lambda)}}.

Definition 3.16

(Specialisation for coloured Alexander polynomials)

The associated specialisation of coefficients is given by:

\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}:\mathbb{C}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},...,u_{l}^{\pm 1},x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}},...,\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm\frac{\lambda_{l}}{2}}]

(3.15)

\begin{cases}&\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}(u_{j})=\left(\psi_{\bar{N}}(x_{j})\right)^{1-\mathcal{N}}=\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{(1-\mathcal{N})\lambda_{i}}\\
&\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}(x_{i})=\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\lambda_{i}},\ i\in\{1,...,l\}\\
&\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}(y)=([\lambda_{C(1)}]_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}),\\
&\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}(d)=\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{-1}.\end{cases}

3.4
Specialisations of coefficients for universal invariants

Definition 3.17

(Rings for the universal invariant)

Let us define the following rings:

(3.16)

\begin{cases}\mathbb{L}:=\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\\
\Lambda^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}=\mathbb{Z}[d^{\pm 1}].\end{cases}

Definition 3.18

(Specialisation for the universal invariant)

We change the ring of coefficients as above, and let us define the associated globalised specialisation of coefficients as below:

\psi^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}:\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\rightarrow\Lambda^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}

(3.17)

\psi^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}(x)=d^{1-\mathcal{N}}.

Definition 3.19

(Universal specialisation map, as in (
8.9
))

We have the projective limit of the sequence of rings:

\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}:=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}.

Then, we have a well-defined induced universal specialisation map, which we denote:

(3.18)

\psi^{u,J}_{\mathcal{N}}:\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}\rightarrow\Lambda^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}.

4
Homological set-up

Let us consider
n,\bar{l}\in\mathbb{N}
.
We denote by
\mathscr{D}_{2n+1,\bar{l}}
the
(2n+\bar{l}+1)
-punctured disc where the set of punctures is spit as follows:

•

2n
punctures placed horizontally, called
p
-punctures (denoted by
\{1,..,2n\}
)

•

1
puncture called
q
-puncture (labeled by
\{0\}
)

•

\bar{l}
punctures placed as in Figure
4.1
, called
s
-punctures (labeled by
\it{\{1,...,\overline{l}\}}
).

Convention 4.1

If
\bar{l}=1
, which will be the case for our models from this paper, we will remove it from the indices of our configuration spaces and homology groups.

4.1
Configuration space of the punctured disc

Now, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
we consider the unordered configuration space of
m
points in the punctured disc
\mathscr{D}_{2n+1,\bar{l}}
, and denoted it by:

C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}:=\mathrm{Conf}_{m}(\mathscr{D}_{2n+1,\bar{l}}).

We fix a base point of this configuration space, by choosing
d_{1},..d_{m}\in\partial\hskip 1.42262pt\mathscr{D}_{2n+1,\bar{l}}
and consider
{\bf d}=(d_{1},...,d_{m})
to be the associated point in the configuration space. In the following part we construct a local system on the space
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}
.

We assume that
m\geqslant 2
. Then, let us start with the abelianisation to the first homology group of this configuration space, which has the following form.

Proposition 4.2

(Abelianisation to the homology group)

Let
[\ ]:\pi_{1}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}})\rightarrow H_{1}\left(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}\right)
be the abelianisation of the fundamental group of our configuration space. Its homology has the structure presented below:

\displaystyle H_{1}\left(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}\right)\simeq

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}\ \ \ \ \oplus\ \ \ \ \mathbb{Z}^{n}\ \ \ \ \ \oplus\ \ \ \ \mathbb{Z}^{\bar{l}}\ \ \ \ \oplus\ \ \ \ \mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\langle[\sigma_{i}]\rangle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \langle[\bar{\sigma}_{i^{\prime}}]\rangle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \langle[\gamma_{j}]\rangle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \langle[\delta]\rangle,\ \ \ {i\in\{0,...,n\}},j\in\{1,...,\bar{l}\},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle i^{\prime}\in\{1,...,n\}}.

The five types of generators are presented in the picture below.

Figure 4.1
:
Local system

4.2
Local system and covering space at level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}

Definition 4.3

(Augmentation map)

After this first step, we consider the following augmentation map

\nu:H_{1}\left(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{\bar{l}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}

\hskip 79.66771pt\langle x_{i}\rangle\ \ \langle\bar{x_{i}}\rangle\ \ \langle y_{j}\rangle\ \ \langle\bar{d^{\prime}}\rangle

defined by the formulas:

(4.1)

\begin{cases}&\nu(\sigma_{0})=0\\
&\nu(\sigma_{i})=2x_{i},\\
&\nu(\bar{\sigma}_{i})=2\bar{x}_{i},i\in\{1,...,n\}\\
&\nu(\gamma_{j})=y_{j},j\in\{1,...,\bar{l}\}\\
&\nu(\delta)=\bar{d^{\prime}}.\end{cases}

Definition 4.4

(The first local system)
Let us consider the local system that is given by the composition of the two morphisms from above:

(4.2)

\displaystyle\Phi:\pi_{1}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{\bar{l}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\langle x_{i}\rangle\ \ \langle\bar{x_{i}}\rangle\ \ \langle y_{j}\rangle\ \ \langle\bar{d^{\prime}}\rangle,\ i\in\{1,...,n\},\ j\in\{1,...,\bar{l}\}

\displaystyle\Phi=\nu\circ[\ ].\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

In the next part we continue with a smaller local system, which will depend on a choice of a sequence of “levels”. This will be used in order to make sure that our submanifolds which have geometric supports given by configuration spaces on circles lift to the covering associated to this local system.

Definition 4.5

(Multi-level)

Let us fix a sequence of levels
\mathcal{N}_{1},...,\mathcal{N}_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
and consider a “multi-level” associated to this set, given by:

(4.3)

\bar{\mathcal{N}}:=(\mathcal{N}_{1},...,\mathcal{N}_{n}).

Definition 4.6

(
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
-change of variables)
For a multi-level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
, let us consider the morphism

(4.4)

\displaystyle f^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{\bar{l}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{\bar{l}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\langle x_{i}\rangle\ \ \langle\bar{x_{i}}\rangle\ \ \langle y_{j}\rangle\ \ \langle\bar{d^{\prime}}\rangle\hskip 5.69054pt\langle x_{i}\rangle\ \ \langle y_{j}\rangle\ \ \langle d^{\prime}\rangle,\ i\in\{1,...,n\},\ j\in\{1,...,\bar{l}\}

given by:

(4.5)

\begin{cases}&f^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}(x_{i})=x_{i},\\
&f^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}(\bar{x_{i}})=x_{i}+(\mathcal{N}_{i}-1)d^{\prime},\\
&f^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}(y_{j})=y_{j},\\
&f^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}(\bar{d^{\prime}})=2d^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Definition 4.7

(
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
-change of variables at the level of the group ring)
Let us define the morphism induced by
f^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
at the level of group rings:

(4.6)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},\bar{x}_{1}^{\pm 1},...,\bar{x}_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},\bar{d^{\prime}}^{\pm 1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\prime\pm 1}]

given by:

(4.7)

\begin{cases}&\tilde{f}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}(x_{i})=x_{i},\\
&\tilde{f}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}(\bar{x_{i}})=x_{i}\cdot d^{\prime(\mathcal{N}_{i}-1)},\\
&\tilde{f}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}(y_{j})=y_{j},\\
&\tilde{f}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}(\bar{d^{\prime}})=d^{\prime 2}.\end{cases}

Definition 4.8

(Local system associated to a multi-level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
)
Let us consider the local system that is given by the composition of the two morphisms from above:

(4.8)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}:\pi_{1}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{\bar{l}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\langle x_{i}\rangle\ \ \langle y_{j}\rangle\ \ \langle d^{\prime}\rangle,\ i\in\{1,...,n\},\ j\in\{1,...,\bar{l}\}

\displaystyle\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}=f^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}\circ\Phi.\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

Definition 4.9

(Covering space at multi-level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
)

Let
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
be the covering of the configuration space
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}
which is associated to the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
local system
\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
.

Notation 4.10

(Base point)

For the next steps we also fix a base point
\tilde{{\bf d}}
which belongs to the fiber over
{\bf d}
in
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
.

4.3
Passing from groups to group rings

We will work with the homologies of the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
covering space. More precisely, our tools will use a Poincaré-Lefschetz duality between two homology groups of
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
. In order to define this duality, we pass from groups to group rings of coefficients, as follows.

We do this in two steps. First, we will enlarge the group associated to the image of the local system
\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
. Secondly, we will use a change of variables which will play an important role for computations.

We remark that the group of deck transformations of the covering space
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
is:

\mathrm{Im}\left(\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}\right)=(2\mathbb{Z})^{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{\bar{l}}\oplus(2\mathbb{Z})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{\bar{l}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}.

This means that the homology of this covering space
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
is a module over the associated group ring:

\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 2},...,x_{n}^{\pm 2},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\prime\pm 2}].

Definition 4.11

(Inclusion of group rings)

Let us denote the following inclusion map:

\iota:\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 2},...,x_{n}^{\pm 2},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\prime\pm 4}]\subseteq\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\prime\pm 1}].

We consider the homology of the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
covering tensored over
\iota
with the group ring

\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\prime\pm 1}].

Using this change of coefficients, we have homology groups which become modules over:

\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\prime\pm 1}].

For the next part, we want to change further the variables by multiplying with the
4^{th}
root of unity
\xi_{2}=i=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{4}}
. This will play an important role in the actual computations, which we explain below.

Notation 4.12

(Variables used for computations encoding orientations)

Let us consider a new variable:

(4.9)

d:=\xi_{2}d^{\prime}.

Accordingly, via the function
\tilde{f}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
, this notation corresponds to the operation given by rising to the
2^{nd}
power, so this corresponds to the change of variables:

(4.10)

\bar{d}:=-\bar{d^{\prime}}.

From now we will use this variable, having in mind certain computations of the intersection pairing.

Explanation
More specifically, our intersection pairing will be computed for the situation where the homology classes are given by submanifolds in the configuration space in the punctured disc. For the actual computation we will use a property that allows us to encode the sign of the geometric intersections in the configuration space by signs of intersections in the punctured disc, by replacing
\bar{d^{\prime}}
by the variable
-\bar{d^{\prime}}
(which we denote by
\bar{d}
, and we refer to
[
4
, Remark 3.4.3]
and
[
11
, Section 3]
for a precise explanation of the sign formula). Correspondingly, after passing from
\bar{d^{\prime}}
to
\bar{d}
this means to replace
d^{\prime}
by

d:=-id^{\prime}

in the computations for the intersection pairing which we will do in the next sections.

Remark 4.13

(Computing the intersection pairing–relative twisting)

With these notations, when computing the intersection pairing for the two topological models from the next sections (see relations (
5.8
), (
5.8
)) we will count
d^{2}
for the contribution of the relative twisting.

This motivates the next change of variables that we introduce below.

Definition 4.14

(Change of variables used for orientation purposes)

Let us consider:

(4.11)

\displaystyle\eta:\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\prime\pm 1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\eta(x_{i})=x_{i}\\
\eta(y_{j})=y_{j}\\
\eta(d^{\prime})=id.\end{cases}

Remark 4.15

(Structure of the homology of the level
\mathcal{N}
covering space)

Using this change of coefficients, the homology groups of the level
\mathcal{N}
covering space
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
become modules over the following ring:

(4.12)

\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}].

4.4
Homology groups associated to a level
\mathcal{N}

Our homological tools will use the relative homology of the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
covering space in the middle dimension. The definitions from this subsection rely on the construction of certain homology groups coming from a particular choice of splitting the boundary of coverings of configuration spaces from
[
9
]
. However, as we will see in the next subsection, we will use a very concrete way of computing intersection pairings in these homologies, which we explain in detail in Subsection
4.5
.

Notation 4.16

a) Let
S^{-}\subseteq\partial\mathscr{D}_{2n+1,\bar{l}}
be the semicircle on the boundary of the punctured disc containing the points with negative
x
-coordinate. We also fix a point on the boundary of the disc, denoted:

w\in S^{-}\subseteq\partial\mathscr{D}_{2n+1,\bar{l}}.

b) Let us denote by
C^{-}
the subspace in the boundary of the configuration space
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}
given by configurations where at least one point is in the set
S^{-}
.

c) Also, let
P^{-}\subseteq\partial C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
be the part of the boundary of level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
-covering given by the fiber over
C^{-}
.

In the next part, we consider two homology groups which will involve submodules in the Borel-Moore homology of the covering space
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
, given by the twisted Borel-Moore homology of the base space
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}
(twisted by the local system
\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
).

Definition 4.17

For the definition of the two homology groups, we split the infinity part of the configuration space in two parts. The precise procedure for this construction is described in
[
9
, Remark 7.5]
(for the case of the
2
-dimensional disc minus half of its boundary and minus
2n+{\bar{l}}+1
open discs with pairwise disjoint closures).

Using this splitting, we define two homology groups as follows.

\displaystyle\bullet\text{ We consider the homology group }H^{\text{lf},\infty,-}_{m}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}},P^{-};\mathbb{Z})\text{ which is given}

\displaystyle\text{by the homology relative to part of the infinity that is the open boundary of}

\displaystyle C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}}\text{ consisting in configurations that project to a multipoint in }C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}

\displaystyle\text{ that touches a puncture from }\mathscr{D}_{2n+1,\bar{l}}\text{ and also relative to the boundary }P^{-}.

\displaystyle\bullet\text{Also, let us define }H^{lf,\Delta}_{m}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}},\partial;\mathbb{Z})\text{ to be the homolgy relative to }

\displaystyle\text{ the boundary of the covering space which is not in }P^{-}\text{and Borel-Moore}

\displaystyle\text{ with respect to collisions of particles from the configuration space}.

Remark 4.18

In the next part we will see that the Borel-Moore homology of a covering space is different from the twisted Borel-Moore homology of the base space.
For our construction, we will use the homology groups of the covering space rather than the twisted homology of the base space. For this purpose, we use the following properties.

Proposition 4.19

(
[
9
]
, Theorem E)

Let
\mathscr{L}_{\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}}
be the rank
1
local system associated to
\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
(as in
[
9
, Definition 2.7]
). Then there are natural injective maps between the following homologies:

(4.13)

\displaystyle\iota:H^{\text{lf},\infty,-}_{m}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}},C^{-};\mathscr{L}_{\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}})\rightarrow H^{\text{lf},\infty,-}_{m}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}},P^{-};\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle\iota^{\partial}:H^{\text{lf},\Delta}_{m}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}},\partial;\mathscr{L}_{\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}})\rightarrow H^{lf,\Delta}_{m}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}},\partial;\mathbb{Z}).

We will use the images of the twisted homologies of the base configuration space from Proposition
4.19
, seen in the homologies of the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
covering
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
.

Definition 4.20

(Homology of the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
covering)

We denote the submodules in the homologies of the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
covering space that are the images of the inclusions
\iota
and
\iota^{\partial}
respectively:

\bullet

\mathscr{H}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}\subseteq H^{\text{lf},\infty,-}_{m}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}},P^{-1};\mathbb{Z})
and

\bullet

\mathscr{H}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}\subseteq H^{\text{lf},\Delta}_{m}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}},\partial;\mathbb{Z})
.

As we have seen in (
4.12
), all these homologies are
\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]
-modules.

These two homologies are related by a geometric intersection pairing that comes from a Poincaré-Lefschetz type duality for twisted homology (see
[
9
, Proposition 3.2]
] and
[
9
, Lemma 3.3]
).

Proposition 4.21

(
[
9
, Proposition 7.6]
)
There is a topological intersection pairing between the following homology groups:

\ll~,~\gg:\mathscr{H}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}\otimes\mathscr{H}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}].

4.5
Computation of the geometric intersection pairing

We will need to perform precise computations of this intersection pairing, so in this subsection we sketch the main steps needed for its formula (that is described in
[
9
, Section 7]
). We will work with the coefficients that belong to the group ring from equation (
4.12
). For this, we introduce the following notation.

Notation 4.22

(Passing to the group ring and encode orientations)

A

Let
\tilde{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
be the morphism induced by the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
local system
\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
, that takes values in the group ring of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{\bar{l}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
:

(4.14)

\tilde{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}:\pi_{1}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\prime\pm 1}].

Then, using the change of variables
\eta
, we consider:

(4.15)

\displaystyle\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}:\pi_{1}(C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]

\displaystyle\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}=\eta\circ\tilde{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}.\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

In the next part we present the formula for the intersection pairing, which will use this morphism.
Let
H_{1}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}
and
H_{2}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}
be two homology classes. Moreover, let us suppose that these two classes are given by two lifts
\tilde{X}_{1},\tilde{X}_{2}
of immersed submanifolds in the base space, denoted
X_{1},X_{2}\subseteq C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}
. We assume that
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
intersect transversely, in a finite number of points.

The intersection pairing will be computed following two steps.

1) Loop associated to an intersection point
First, we associate to each intersection point
x\in X_{1}\cap X_{2}
a loop in the configuration space
l_{x}\subseteq C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}
. After that, we will grade this via the local system and the morphism
\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
.

Definition 4.23

(Construction of
l_{x}
)

For
x\in X_{1}\cap X_{2}
, we suppose that we have the paths
\gamma_{X_{1}},\gamma_{X_{2}}
starting in
\bf d
, ending on
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
respectively such that

\tilde{\gamma}_{X_{1}}(1)\in\tilde{X}_{1}
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{X_{2}}(1)\in\tilde{X}_{2}
. Here,
\tilde{\gamma}_{X_{i}}
is the unique lift of
{\gamma}_{X_{i}}
through
\tilde{\bf d}
.
Let us choose
\nu_{X_{1}},\nu_{X_{2}}:[0,1]\rightarrow C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}
two paths with the properties:

(4.16)

\begin{cases}Im(\nu_{X_{1}})\subseteq X_{1};\nu_{X_{1}}(0)=\gamma_{X_{1}}(1);\nu_{X_{1}}(1)=x\\
Im(\nu_{X_{2}})\subseteq X_{2};\nu_{X_{2}}(0)=\gamma_{X_{2}}(1);\nu_{x_{2}}(1)=x.\end{cases}

The composition of these paths gives us the loop:

l_{x}=\gamma_{X_{1}}\circ\nu_{X_{1}}\circ\nu_{X_{2}}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{X_{2}}^{-1}.

2) Grade the family of loops using the local system

Proposition 4.24

(Intersection pairing from graded intersections in the base space)

The intersection pairing can be computed from the set of loops
l_{x}
and the local system:

(4.17)

\ll H_{1},H_{2}\gg=\eta\left(\sum_{x\in X_{1}\cap X_{2}}\alpha_{x}\cdot\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}(l_{x})\right)\in\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]

where
\alpha_{x}
is the sign of the geometric intersection between the submanifolds
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
at the point
x
, in the configuration space
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l}}
.

Remark 4.25

(Submanifolds coming from products of manifolds of dimension one)

If the homology classes come from products of one dimensional submanifolds in the punctured disc, quotiented to the unordered configuration space, we can compute directly the intersection
\ll H_{1},H_{2}\gg
from the sum presented in equation (
4.17
) without the change of coefficients given by
\eta
.

In this situation we can replace the local system
\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
by
\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
in the previous formula, counting the sign contribution given just by the product of local orientations in the disc around each component of the intersection point
x
(instead of computing the orientation in the configuration space given by
\alpha_{x}
).

We will use this type of computations in Section
5
and Section
6
.

4.6
Specialisations given by colorings

So far, the construction of the two homology groups is intrinsec and does not depend on a braid representative that gives a link. In this part we will prepare our homological tools for the situation where we have a braid with
n
-strands that gives a link with
l
components by braid closure. This will induce a colouring, as below.

Definition 4.26

(Colouring of the punctures
C
)

Let
C
be a coloring of the
2n

p
-punctures of the disc
\{1,...,2n\}
by
l
colours, as below:

(4.18)

C:\{1,...,2n\}\rightarrow\{1,...,l\}.

There are two special situations that occur in our models, namely
{\bar{l}}=1
or
{\bar{l}}=l
. In this paper, we have the case
{\bar{l}}=1
. In the sequel paper for
3
-manifold invariants we will use
{\bar{l}}=l
.

We fix the last
p
-puncture and denote it by
\bar{p}=2n
. We will position the
\bar{l}=1

s
-puncture underneath this special
p
-puncture labeled by
\bar{p}
. Using this, we define the colour of the
s
-punctures as being the same as the colour of
\bar{p}
(which is induced by the colouring
C
).

Definition 4.27

(Change of coefficients
f_{C}
)

We change the variables associated to the punctures of the punctured disc using the colouring
C
. More specifically, let us change the first
n+{\bar{l}}
variables
x_{1},...,x_{n},y_{1},...,y_{\bar{l}}
from the ring
\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]
to
l+{\bar{l}}
variables, which we denote by
x_{1},..,x_{l},y_{1},...,y_{\bar{l}}
, as below:

f_{C}:\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]

(4.19)

\begin{cases}&f_{C}(x_{i})=x_{C(i)},\ i\in\{1,...,n\}\\
&f_{C}(y_{j})=y_{C(\bar{p}_{j})},\ j\in\{1,...,{\bar{l}}\}.\end{cases}

Now, we look at this change of coefficients at the level of the homology groups, via the function
f_{C}
.

Definition 4.28

(Homology groups) We consider the two homologies over the ring associated to the new coefficients:

\bullet

H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}:=\mathscr{H}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}|_{f_{C}}

\bullet

H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}:=\mathscr{H}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}|_{f_{C}}.

We remark that these homology groups are modules over
\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]
.

Notation 4.29

(Cases when we remove
\bar{l}
from notations)

Since for the models for link invariants we have
\bar{l}=1
, we remove this from the notation of the homology groups, and also replace
y_{1}
by
y
as variable, and use the following notations.

Notation 4.30

(Homology groups for link invariants)

We have defined above the homologies of the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
covering
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
, which we denote by:

H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,m},H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,m}

and which are modules over the ring

\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]
.

4.8
General procedure for encoding homology classes in the base configuration space

Notation 4.31

(Geometric supports and paths to the base points)

In all constructions from now on we will use homology classes in a covering of the configuration space that are prescribed by the following data:

•

A
geometric support
, meaning a
set of arcs in the punctured disc
or a set of
circles in the punctured disc
on which we consider unordered configurations of a prescribed number of particles. The image of the product of these arcs or configurations on circles in the configuration space, gives us a submanifold
F
which has half of the dimension of the configuration space.

•

A collection of
paths connected to the base point
, starting in the base points from the punctured disc and ending on these curves or circles. Then, the collection of these paths gives a path in the configuration space, starting in
\bf d
and ending on the submanifold
F
.

Let us suppose that the submanifold
F
has a well defined lift to the covering.
Then, we lift the path to a path in the covering space, starting from
\tilde{\bf{d}}
and after that we lift the submanifold through the end point of this path. The detailed construction of homology classes via this dictionary is presented in
[
4
, Section 5]
.

We will see explicit examples of such classes in the next sections.

4.9
Lift of submanifolds with support on configuration spaces on circles

In this part we show that if we consider a submanifold which has as geometric support a collection of configuration spaces on symmetric circles, whose multiplicities are prescribed by the multi-level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
, then this submanifold has a well-defined lift in the covering associated to the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
local system.
More specifically, let us fix the following parameters:

n\rightarrow n;\ \ \ m\rightarrow 1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{N}_{i};\ \ \ {\bar{l}};\ \ \ \bar{\mathcal{N}}.

This means that we work in the configuration space of
1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{N}_{i}
particles in the
(2n+2)
-punctured disc:

\mathrm{Conf}_{1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{N}_{i}}\left(\mathbb{D}_{2n+2}\right)

After this, let us consider the local system
\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
associated to the these parameters.

We will use the homology groups associated to this data:

\mathscr{H}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{N}_{i},\bar{l}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mathscr{H}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{N}_{i},\bar{l}}.

Remark 4.32

The following property holds for any
\bar{l}
, even if for the later sections we use
\bar{l}=1
.

Definition 4.33

(Level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
submanifold based on ovals)

Let
L(\bar{\mathcal{N}})
be the submanifold in the configuration space given by the product of configuration spaces on symmetric ovals, with multiplicities as in Figure
4.3
and the circle which goes around the puncture labeled by
0
:

\normalsize\tilde{L}(\bar{\mathcal{N}})

\hskip 14.22636pt\downarrow\text{ lifts }

\normalsize L(\bar{\mathcal{N}})

\hskip 327.20668pt\mathrm{Conf}_{1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{N}_{i}}\left(\mathbb{D}_{2n+2}\right)

Figure 4.3
:
Lifting Lagrangians supported by configurations on ovals

Lemma 4.34

(Lifting submanifolds given by configurations on ovals)

The geometric support based on ovals with multiplicities given by the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
, shown in the Figure
4.3
, leads to a well-defined class in the homology
\mathscr{H}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{N}_{i},\bar{l}}
of the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
-covering space
C_{n,m}^{\bar{l},\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
.

Figure 4.4
:
Monodromy of configurations on a circle

Remark 4.35

In Figure
4.4
we use the local system after we pass to the group ring, so all notations are multiplicative. For the purpose of clarity of signs, in the following proof we will use the additive notation, as below.

Proof.

In order to prove that the geometric support given by configuration spaces on circles lifts to the covering, we will check that the total monodromy on each circle gets evaluated to zero by the local system
\bar{\Phi}^{\mathcal{N}}
.

More precisely, for a fixed
i\in\{1,...,n\}
, the local system
\Phi
counts the monodromy around the symmetric points
(i,2n+1-i)
with variables
2x_{i}
and
2\bar{x}_{i}
.
Now, let us look at a loop given by
\mathcal{N}_{i}
particles that go around a circle, as in Figure
4.4
. The local system
\Phi
(introduced in Definition
4.4
) will count the total monodromy for this circle by the following expression:

(4.20)

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{N}_{i}\cdot x_{i}-2\mathcal{N}_{i}\cdot\bar{x}_{i}+\frac{\mathcal{N}_{i}(\mathcal{N}_{i}-1)}{2}(2\bar{d^{\prime}})=

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{N}_{i}\cdot x_{i}-2\mathcal{N}_{i}\cdot\bar{x}_{i}+\mathcal{N}_{i}(\mathcal{N}_{i}-1)\bar{d^{\prime}}.

Here,
2\mathcal{N}_{i}\cdot x_{i}
comes from the rotation around the puncture
i
,
2\mathcal{N}_{i}\cdot\bar{x}_{i}
from the rotation around the symmetric puncture
2n+1-i
and
\frac{\mathcal{N}_{i}(\mathcal{N}_{i}-1)}{2}(2\bar{d^{\prime}})
from the relative winding of the
\mathcal{N}_{i}
particles along the circle.

We remark that this monodromy does not wanish, so the local system
\Phi
is not enough to give us well defined homology classes.

Further, we look at the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
covering space and its homology groups. For this, we have to change the variables using the function
f^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
(using Definition
4.7
and Definition
4.8
). If we do so, we change the variables in the following way:

(4.21)

\begin{cases}&x_{i}\rightarrow x_{i},\\
&\bar{x_{i}}\rightarrow x_{i}+(\mathcal{N}_{i}-1)d^{\prime},\\
&\bar{d^{\prime}}\rightarrow 2d^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Then, the monodromy of the loop, expressed in relation (
4.20
) gets evaluated to the following:

(4.22)

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{N}_{i}\cdot x_{i}+2\mathcal{N}_{i}\cdot\bar{x}_{i}+\mathcal{N}_{i}(\mathcal{N}_{i}-1)\bar{d^{\prime}}\rightarrow

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{N}_{i}\cdot x_{i}-2\mathcal{N}_{i}\cdot(x_{i}+(\mathcal{N}_{i}-1)d^{\prime})+\mathcal{N}_{i}(\mathcal{N}_{i}-1)2d^{\prime}

\displaystyle=0.

This means that the configuration space on a circle around these symmetric punctures lifts to a well-defined submanifold in the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
covering.
On the other hand, the local system
\Phi
is chosen to have trivial monodromy around the puncture labeled by
0
, so the circle which goes around this puncture has a well-defined lift.

Then, we do this for all configuration spaces on the
n
circles and the circle which goes around the puncture
0
and remark that the total monodromy of such a loop vanishes. This shows that we have a well-defined lift that gives a submanifold
\tilde{L}(\bar{\mathcal{N}})
in the
\bar{\mathcal{N}}-
covering space. Further on, if we fix a base point for lifting it, then it leads to a well-defined homology class in:

\mathscr{H}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{N}_{i},\bar{l}}.

This concludes the lifting property at the level
\mathcal{N}
covering space.
∎

4.10
Braid group action

We will use the set of braids which have
n+1+{\bar{l}}
strands, whose induced action on the punctured disc preserves the colouring of the punctures (induced by
C
). We denote the set of such braids by
B^{C}_{n+{\bar{l}}+1}
.

Proposition 4.36

(
[
9
