]
)

The braid group action coming from the mapping class group action on the punctured disc induces an action on the homology of the level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
-covering, compatible with the deck transformations, as below:

B^{C}_{n+{\bar{l}}+1}\curvearrowright H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}\ \left(\text{as module over the ring }\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\right).

Proposition 4.37

(
[
9
]
Intersection pa1iring)

We have a specialised intersection pairing assoaciated to the above homology groups:

\left\langle~,~\right\rangle:H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}\otimes H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,m,{\bar{l}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y_{1}^{\pm 1}...,y_{\bar{l}}^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}].

The formula for the computation of
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle
is the same as the one from Proposition
4.24
, specialised via
f_{C}
:

\left\langle~,~\right\rangle=\ \ll~,~\gg|_{f_{C}}.

5
Coloured Jones polynomials for framed links

In the next two sections, we construct two topological models: the first one for coloured Jones polynomials for framed links and the second one for coloured Alexander polynomials for framed links.

These two models are different and each of these has its own geometric characteristics. More specifically, the homological set-ups are different and the local systems that we will use are different. In particular, for the case of link invariants, the homology classes for the two quantum invariants
J_{N_{1},...,N_{l}}
and
\Phi^{\mathcal{N}}_{\bar{\lambda}}
cannot be seen in the same homology group which then gets specialised in two different manners in order to recover the two link invariants.

We start with a topological intersection model for coloured Jones polynomials for links, whose components are coloured with different colours. Such a formula is presented in
[
6
]
, where the geometric supports for the second homology class are given by configuration spaces on figure eights. The advantage of this new model is that we construct homology classes using configuration spaces on circles. We will do this by choosing a more subtle local system than the one from
[
6
]
.

Let us consider a framed oriented link
L=K_{1}\cup...\cup K_{l}
with
l
components and framings
f_{1},...,f_{l}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, let
\beta_{n}\in B_{n}
be a braid representative such that
L=\widehat{\beta_{n}}
.

We fix
N_{1},...,N_{l}\in\mathbb{N}
a set of colours and the associated coloured multi-index:

\bar{N}:=(N_{1},...,N_{l}).

In the next part, we will choose the local system such that our geometric support given by configuration spaces on circles leads to a well defined homology class in the homology of that covering.

Notation 5.1

Let
M\in\mathbb{N}
, and denote by
V_{M}
the
M
-dimensional representation of the quantum group
U_{q}(sl(2))
for generic
q
.

In the next part we colour the components of
L
with the representations
V_{N_{1}},...,V_{N_{l}}
. Then, we denote by
J_{\bar{N}}(L,q)
to be the coloured Jones polynomial of this framed link (as defined in
[
24
]
).

Definition 5.2

(Induced colorings)

a) (Colourings associated to the braid) The colouring of
L
via the colours
\bar{N}
gives a colouring of the strands of the braid, and we define the associated colours by:

(C_{1},...,C_{n})
, as in Figure
5.1
:

Figure 5.1
:
Colouring for the braid

After this, we look at the link
L
as being the closure of the braid together with
n
straight strands. This gives a colouring of
2n
points
C:\{1,...,2n\}\rightarrow\{1,...,l\}
.
Let us denote by

(5.1)

N^{C}_{i}:=N_{C(i)}.

We now have the following colours on the
2n
points:

\bar{C}^{\bar{N}}:=(N^{C}_{1},...,N^{C}_{n},N^{C}_{n},...,N^{C}_{1}).

Definition 5.3

(Set of states) Let us define the following indexing set:

C(\bar{N}):=\big\{\bar{i}=(i_{1},...,i_{n-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}\mid 0\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant N^{C}_{k+1}-1,\ \forall k\in\{1,...,n-1\}\big\}.

Definition 5.4

(
\bar{N}
-coloured multi-level)

We consider the following indices:

(5.2)

\begin{cases}&\mathcal{N}_{1}=1,\\
&\mathcal{N}_{i}=N^{C}_{i}-1,i\in\{2,...,n\}.\end{cases}

Then, we define
\bar{N}
-coloured multi-level (associated to the coloured multi-index
\bar{N}
):

(5.3)

\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N}):=(\mathcal{N}_{1},...,\mathcal{N}_{n})\left(=(1,N^{C}_{2}-1,...,N^{C}_{n}-1)\right).

5.1
Homology classes

Now, we use the induced colouring and the associated indexing set
C(\bar{N})
, in order to define the homology groups that we use for the topological model. More precisely, we fix the configuration space of
2+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1)
points on the
(2n+2)
-punctured disc. After this, let us consider the local system
\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N})}
associated to the following parameters:

n\rightarrow n;\ \ \ m\rightarrow 2+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1);\ \ \ {\bar{l}}\rightarrow 1;\ \ \ \bar{\mathcal{N}}\rightarrow\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N}).

We will use the homology groups associated to this data:

H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N})}_{n,2+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1),1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N}),\partial}_{n,2+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1),1}.

For the next part we will erase the third component from the indices of the homology groups.

Now we have all the tools needed in order to introduce the homology classes that we use for the intersection model for coloured Jones polynomials.

Definition 5.5

(Homology classes)

Let
\bar{i}=(i_{1},...,i_{n-1})\in C(\bar{N})
be a multi-index. We construct two homology classes associated to this multi-index, which are given by the geometric supports from Figure
5.2
:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N})}_{n,2+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1)}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N}),\partial}_{n,2+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1)}}.

\hskip 14.22636pt\downarrow\text{ lifts }

Figure 5.2
:

Lagrangians for Coloured Jones link invariants coloured with diferent colours

Proposition 5.6

The homology class
\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N}),\partial}_{n,2+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1)}
is well-defined.

Proof.

The geometric support for the dual class, shown in the right hand side of Figure
5.2
is the same as the geometric support from Definition
4.33
, associated to the multi-level
\mathcal{N}(\bar{N})
, which we denoted by

L(\mathcal{N}(\bar{N})).

Further on, we showed in Lemma
4.34
that this submanifold has well-defined lifts in the covering.

In order to fix the lift, we use the procedure of lifting described in Notation
4.31
. More specifically, we use the path in the configuration space induced by the collection of straight vertical paths which connect the ovals to the boundary of the disc, shown in the right hand side of Figure
5.2
(see
[
4
]
, Section 5 for a similar detailed construction of this path in the configuration space). This is a path from the base point
{\bf d}
to our submanifold
L(\mathcal{N}(\bar{N}))
.

The lift of this path through the base point
\tilde{{\bf d}}
from the covering gives us a base point for lifting the submanifold, and we obtain a lift:

\tilde{L}(\mathcal{N}(\bar{N}))_{\bar{i}}.

Following Lemma
4.34
, the submanifold
\tilde{L}(\mathcal{N}(\bar{N}))_{\bar{i}}
gives a well-defined homology class in the dual homology of the covering
H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N}),\partial}_{n,2+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1)}
, which we denote:

\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N}),\partial}_{n,2+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1)}.

Figure 5.3
:
Monodromy of configurations on a circle

∎

In the next part, we use the specialisation of coefficients from Section
3
, which we remind below.

Definition 5.7

(Specialisations for link invariants)

We use the following specialisation:

\psi_{\bar{N}}:\mathbb{C}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},...,u_{l}^{\pm 1},x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[q^{\pm 1}]

(5.4)

\begin{cases}&\psi_{\bar{N}}(u_{j})=\left(\psi_{\bar{N}}(x_{j})\right)^{t}=q^{N_{i}-1}\\
&\psi_{\bar{N}}(x_{i})=q^{N_{i}-1},\ i\in\{1,...,l\}\\
&\psi_{\bar{N}}(y)=[N^{C}_{1}]_{q},\\
&\psi_{\bar{N}}(d)=q^{-1}.\end{cases}

5.2
Intersection model

In this part we present a topological model for the coloured Jones polynomial of a link coloured with the colours
N_{1},...,N_{l}
. More precisely, we will see that it can be obtained from an intersection pairing which uses the classes
\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}
for all indices
\bar{i}\in C(\bar{N})
, as stated in Theorem
2.8
. We remind the formula below.

Theorem 5.8

(Topological model for coloured Jones polynomials for coloured links via ovals)

(5.5)

\displaystyle J_{N_{1},...,N_{l}}(L,q)

\displaystyle=~\left(J{\Gamma}^{\bar{N}}(\beta_{n})\right)\Bigm|_{\psi_{\bar{N}}}.

where the intersection pairing is given by the formula from below:

(5.6)

\begin{cases}&J{\Gamma}^{\bar{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},...,u_{l}^{\pm 1},x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\\
&J{\Gamma}^{\bar{N}}(\beta_{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{l}u_{i}^{\left(f_{i}-\sum_{j\neq{i}}lk_{i,j}\right)}\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n}x^{-1}_{C(i)}\cdot\\
&\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \sum_{\bar{i}\in C(\bar{N})}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle.\end{cases}

Proof.

Using the expression of the specialisation of coefficients from Section
3
, we see that we have to prove the following formula:

(5.7)

\displaystyle J_{N_{1},...,N_{l}}(L,q)

\displaystyle=~q^{\sum_{i=1}^{l}\left(f_{i}-\sum_{j\neq i}lk_{i,j}\right)(N_{i}-1)}\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\sum_{\bar{i}\in C(\bar{N})}\left(\prod_{i=2}^{n}x^{-1}_{C(i)}\right)\cdot\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle\right)\Bigm|_{\psi_{\bar{N}}}.

The proof of this intersection formula will be done in
4
main steps, which are similar to the ones used in the model for coloured Jones polynomials presented in
[
6
]
. However, the geometry of these classes is totally different than the one from
[
6
]
which uses figure eights as geometric supports. Let us outline the main steps as below.

Step 1

Let us define two homology classes

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}^{\prime}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N})}_{n,1+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1)}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}^{\prime}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N}),\partial}_{n,1+\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1)}}

which are obtained from the geometric supports which are the same as the geometric supports of the classes
\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}
, the only change being that we remove the 1-dimensional part supported around the puncture of disc that is labeled by
0
( more specifically, we remove the purple segment and the blue circle), as in Figure
5.4
:

Figure 5.4
:
Removing the middle circle from the geometric support

Then we obtain the following relation between the intersection pairings (using
d^{2}
for the contribution of the relative twisting, as in Remark
4.13
):

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle=d^{-2\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}i_{k}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle

This means that we want to prove the following:

(5.8)

\displaystyle J_{N_{1},...,N_{l}}(L,q)

\displaystyle=~q^{\sum_{i=1}^{l}\left(f_{i}-\sum_{j\neq i}lk_{i,j}\right)(N_{i}-1)}\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\sum_{\bar{i}\in C(\bar{N})}\prod_{i=2}^{n}x^{-1}_{C(i)}\cdot d^{-2\sum_{k=1}^{n}i_{k}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle\right)\Bigm|_{\psi_{\bar{N}}}.

Step 2

Next, we consider the homology classes given by the support of
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}
and
\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}
where we remove the arc and circle that end or go around the last puncture, as in Figure
5.5
:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N})}_{n,\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1)}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{N}),\partial}_{n,\sum_{i=2}^{n}(N^{C}_{i}-1)}}.

\hskip 14.22636pt\downarrow\text{ lifts }

Figure 5.5
:
Removing the middle circle and the extremal circle from the geometric support

Now, we compare two graded intersections: the one from Step 1, namely

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle

and the new pairing between the classes from Step 2, given by:

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle.

The only difference between these two intersections is that the first one comes with an extra coefficient associated to the intersection point between the extremal circle and the arc ending in the puncture labeled by
2n
, as in Figure
5.6
.

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle

Figure 5.6
:
Removing the middle circle and the extremal circle from the geometric support

This coefficient is the monodromy of the loop presented in the left hand side of Figure
5.6
(which goes around the
s
-puncture), which is
y
. So, we obtain the following relation between these two pairings:

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle=y\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle.

Now, we specialise these intersections using the change of coefficients
\psi_{\bar{N}}
. Using that

\psi_{\bar{N}}(y)=[N^{C}_{1}]_{q}

we obtain the following relation:

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi_{\bar{N}}}=[N^{C}_{1}]_{q}\cdot\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi_{\bar{N}}}

Step 3

Now we use the definition of coloured Jones polynomials via the Reshetikhin-Turaev construction. More precisely, the cups of the diagram on the algebraic side correspond exactly to the sum of the classes
\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}
over all choices of indices
\bar{i}\in C(\bar{N})
.
Further on, the braid action on the quantum side and on the homological side correspond, using the identification due to Martel
[
20
]
.

Step 4

On the last level of the braid closure, on the algebraic side, we evaluate the caps of the diagram. This means that we evaluate non-trivially just the components which are symmetric (excluding the first component, which is associated to the open strand).
On the homological side, this translates in the requirement that we evaluate non-trivially just the homology classes that are symmetric with respect to the puncture
0
, located in the middle of the disc.

Step 5

This condition tells us that the indices associated to the punctures
k
and
2n+1-k
should have the sum
N^{C}_{k}-1
(which is imposed by the colour of the link component associated to the puncture
k
). Geometrically we obtain this property by intersecting with the dual class
\mathscr{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}
.

Step 6

Algebraically, we have to encode an additional piece of data, which is a coefficient corresponding to the caps of the diagram. This comes from the quantum trace from the representation side. The details of the argument for a single colour are discussed in details in
[
3
]
(Section 5 and Section 7). We refer to that argument and discuss below the difference that appears in this context, where we have different colours for our link.

The quantum trace encodes a coefficient that comes from the pivotal structure, which in this situation is given by the action of the element
K^{-1}
of the quantum group (see
[
3
]
for the precise definition).

Let us fix a set of indices
i_{1},...,i_{n-1}
as above. The action of
K^{-1}
on the associated monomial is the following:

(5.9)

q^{-\sum_{k=2}^{n}\left((N^{C}_{k}-1)-2i_{k-1}\right)}=\left(\prod_{k=2}^{n}q^{-(N^{C}_{k}-1)}\right)\cdot q^{\ \sum_{k=1}^{n-1}2i_{k}}.

Now we we would like to see this coefficient as coming from the specialisation of the variables that we use for the local system that gives our homology groups. For this, we remark the following:

(5.10)

\displaystyle\psi_{\bar{N}}(x_{C(i)})=q^{N_{C(i)}-1}=q^{N^{C}_{i}-1},\forall i\in\{1,...,n\}\ (\text{following }\eqref{eq:N})

\displaystyle\psi_{\bar{N}}(d^{-2})=q^{2}.

This means that the coefficient from (
5.9
) is precisely the specialisation:

(5.11)

\psi_{\bar{N}}\left(\left(\prod_{i=2}^{n}x^{-1}_{C(i)}\right)\cdot d^{-2\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}i_{k}}\right).

The last coefficient from the formula presented in (
5.5
) is given by the framing contribution of the components of our link
L
. This completes the intersection model for the coloured Jones polynomial
J_{N_{1},...,N_{l}}(L,q)
for the link coloured with colours
N_{1},...,N_{l}
.
∎

6
Coloured Alexander polynomials for framed links

In this part we prove a topological intersection model for the coloured Alexander polynomials for links.

This is the first topological model for these non-semisimple invariants for links whose components are coloured with different colours. For the case of links coloured with a single colour we showed a topological intersection model in
[
3
]
and
[
5
]
, where we used configuration spaces on figure eights and open circles respectively as the geometric supports for the second homology class.

From the representation theory point of view, the change from the single colour case to the multi-colour case is totally non-trivial and requires the definition of so-called modified dimensions. In order to provide this model, we will encode geometrically this extra data, in our configuration space in the punctured disc.

Even for the case where we colour by a single colour we obtain a new perspective, given by the fact that we have homology classes based on configuration spaces on circles rather than an open support given by the circles minus a puncture (as in
[
5
]
). This is achieved by our work on choosing a more subtle local system than the one from
[
5
]
.

As in the previous section, we fix a framed oriented link
L=K_{1}\cup...\cup K_{l}
with
l
components, which has framings
f_{1},...,f_{l}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then we consider
\beta_{n}\in B_{n}
to be a braid representative such that
L=\widehat{\beta_{n}}
. Also, let
\mathcal{N}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{N}\geqslant 2
. We use the following notation for the set of generic colours:
\ddot{\mathbb{C}}:=(\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z})\cup\mathcal{N}\mathbb{Z}
.

Let
\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{l}\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
a set of colours for the strands of our link. The associated coloured multi-index is given by:

\bar{\lambda}:=(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{l}).

Notation 6.1

For
\lambda\in\ddot{\mathbb{C}}
let us denote by
U_{\lambda}
the associated representation of the quantum group at roots of unity
U_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}(sl(2))
(here
\xi_{\mathcal{N}}
is our fixed primitive root of unity).

Then, we colour the components of
L
with the representations
U_{\lambda_{1}},...,U_{\lambda_{l}}
. Further, let us denote by
\Phi_{\mathcal{N}}(L,\bar{\lambda})
to be the coloured Alexander polynomial of the framed link
L
(which was defined in
[
1
]
).

Now we want to define the local system such that our geometric support given by configuration spaces on circles leads to a well defined homology class in the homology of the associated covering space.

Definition 6.2

(Induced colorings)

a) (Colourings associated to the braid) As in the case of the coloured Jones polynomials, the colours of the link
\bar{\lambda}
induce a colouring of the strands of
\beta_{n}
. Let us denote the associated colours by:

(C_{1},...,C_{n})
, as in Figure
6.1
:

Figure 6.1
:
Colouring for the braid

Now, our link
L
is the closure of
\beta_{n}
together with
n
straight strands. They induce an associated colouring of
2n
points
C:\{1,...,2n\}\rightarrow\{1,...,l\}
.
Let us denote by

(6.1)

\lambda^{C}_{i}:=\lambda_{C(i)}.

We consider the colouring of the
2n
points:

\bar{C}^{\bar{\lambda}}:=(\lambda^{C}_{1},...,\lambda^{C}_{n},\lambda^{C}_{n},...,\lambda^{C}_{1}).

Definition 6.3

(Set of states at level
\mathcal{N}
) In this case, our indexing set will be given by the following set:

C(\mathcal{N}):=\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}=\big\{\bar{i}=(i_{1},...,i_{n-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}\mid 0\leqslant i_{k}\leqslant\mathcal{N}-1,\ \forall k\in\{1,...,n-1\}\big\}.

We remark that in contrast to the case of coloured Jones polynomials, where the set of states depends on the choice of colours (as in Definition
5.3
), here the set of states is intrinsec and it is determined just by the level and not by the particular choice of colours.

Definition 6.4

(
\mathcal{N}
-coloured multi-level)

We consider the following indices:

(6.2)

\begin{cases}&\mathcal{N}_{1}=1,\\
&\mathcal{N}_{i}=\mathcal{N}-1,i\in\{2,...,n\}.\end{cases}

Then, we define
\mathcal{N}
-coloured multi-level (which has
n
components):

(6.3)

\bar{\mathcal{N}}:=(1,\mathcal{N}-1,...,\mathcal{N}-1).

6.1
Homology classes

In the next part we define the homology classes that we work with for this second case: invariants at roots of unity. In contrast to the situation for coloured Jones polynomials, where we used the induced colouring in order to define the homology groups, here we need just the level
\mathcal{N}
in order to define these homologies.

More specifically, let us consider the configuration space of

2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)

points on the
(2n+2)
-punctured disc.

After this, we look at the local system
\Phi^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
associated to the following parameters:

n\rightarrow n;\ \ \ m\rightarrow 2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1);\ \ \ {\bar{l}}\rightarrow 1;\ \ \ \bar{\mathcal{N}}.

We fix the homology groups associated to these parameters, as below:

H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}.

As in the previous section, we erase the third component associated to
\bar{l}
from the indices of the homology groups.

Now we have all the tools needed in order to introduce the homology classes that we use for the intersection model for coloured Alexander polynomials.

Definition 6.5

(Homology classes for ADO invariants)

Let us fix a multi-index
\bar{i}=(i_{1},...,i_{n})\in C(\mathcal{N})
. We define the following homology classes associated to this multi-index, given by the geometric supports from Figure
6.2
:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}}.

\hskip 14.22636pt\downarrow\text{ lifts }

Figure 6.2
:
Lagrangians for Coloured Alexander link invariants coloured with diferent colours

Proposition 6.6

The geometric support presented in Figure
6.2
, induces a well-defined homology class in the covering, which we denoted by
\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}
.

Proof.

We remark that the geometric support for the dual class from Figure
6.2
is the the geometric support from Definition
4.33
, associated to the multi-level
\bar{\mathcal{N}}.

This was denoted by
L(\bar{\mathcal{N}})
. Then, using Lemma
4.34
we know that this submanifold gives well-defined lifts in the associated covering space.

As in the previous section, we use the procedure of lifting described in Notation
4.31
. This means that the collection of straight vertical paths which connect the ovals to the boundary of the disc gives us a path in the configuration space, from
{\bf d}
to
L(\bar{\mathcal{N}})
, shown in the right hand side of Figure
6.2
(see
[
4
]
-Section 5 for the procedure of constructing such a path).

The base point of the covering,
\tilde{{\bf d}}
, gives us a base point for lifting the submanifold, and we obtain a lift denoted by:

\tilde{L}(\bar{\mathcal{N}})_{\bar{i}}.

From Lemma
4.34
, this submanifold gives a well-defined homology class in the dual homology
H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}
. We denote this class by:

\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}.

Figure 6.3
:
Monodromy of configurations on a circle for the coloured Alexander classes

This concludes the proof of the existence of the well-defined homology class.
∎

Now, let us fix the indices:

(6.4)

\begin{cases}\bar{\lambda}:=(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{l})\\
\overline{d(\lambda)}:=([\lambda_{C(1)}]_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}})\\
\end{cases}

We then consider the specialisation of coefficients (which is associated to the multi-indices
\bar{\alpha}=\bar{\lambda}
and
\eta=\overline{d(\lambda)}
), as in Definition
3.16
:

\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}:\mathbb{C}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},...,u_{l}^{\pm 1},x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}},...,\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm\frac{\lambda_{l}}{2}}]

(6.5)

\begin{cases}&\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}(u_{j})=\left(\psi_{\bar{N}}(x_{j})\right)^{1-\mathcal{N}}=\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{(1-\mathcal{N})\lambda_{i}}\\
&\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}(x_{i})=\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{\lambda_{i}},\ i\in\{1,...,l\}\\
&\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}(y)=([\lambda_{C(1)}]_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}),\\
&\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}(d)=\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{-1}.\end{cases}

6.2
Intersection model

In this section we will prove the topological model for the coloured Alexander polynomials of a link coloured with the colours
\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{l}
. We show that this multivariable invariant can be read off from an intersection pairing via the homology classes

\{\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}},\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}\}\text{ for all indices }\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}.

We recall the model presented in Theorem
2.6
.

Theorem 6.7

(Topological model for coloured Alexander polynomials for coloured links via ovals)

We have the following topological model for a link coloured with colours
\bar{\lambda}
:

(6.6)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathcal{N}}_{\bar{\lambda}}(L)

\displaystyle=~\left(\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\right)\Bigm|_{\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}}.

Here, the intersection is given by:

(6.7)

\begin{cases}&\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},...,u_{l}^{\pm 1},x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\\
&\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{l}u_{i}^{\left(f_{i}-\sum_{j\neq{i}}lk_{i,j}\right)}\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n}u^{-1}_{C(i)}\cdot\\
&\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle.\end{cases}

Proof.

First of all, we look at the change of coefficients described in Section
3
, and using this we want to show the following:

(6.8)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathcal{N}}_{\bar{\lambda}}(L)

\displaystyle=~{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}^{\sum_{i=1}^{l}\left(f_{i}-\sum_{j\neq i}lk_{i,j}\right)\lambda_{i}(1-N_{i})}\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}}\left(\prod_{i=2}^{n}u^{-1}_{C(i)}\right)\cdot\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle\right)\Bigm|_{\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}}.

We will prove this model in
6
main parts. The first two steps are purely topological and for the rest we use the connection to representation theory. The strategy has some similarities to the one used for the model for coloured Jones polynomials from the previous section, but there are certain important topological differences, which we will emphasise in the next part.

Step 1

We consider the homology classes obtained from the geometric supports of the classes
\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}
, but where we remove the 1-dimensional part supported around the puncture of the disc labeled by
0
(this means that we remove the purple segment and the blue central circle), as in Figure
6.4
:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,1+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,1+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}}

Figure 6.4
:
Removing the middle circle from the geometric support

Counting the contribution of the intersection point between the circle and the middle arc, this gives the following relation between the intersection pairings (using
d^{2}
for the contribution of the relative twisting, as in Remark
4.13
):

(6.9)

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle=d^{-2\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}i_{k}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle

This means that we want to prove the following:

(6.10)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathcal{N}}_{\bar{\lambda}}(L)

\displaystyle=~{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}^{\sum_{i=1}^{l}\left(f_{i}-\sum_{j\neq i}lk_{i,j}\right)\lambda_{i}(1-N_{i})}\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}}\left(\prod_{i=2}^{n}u^{-1}_{C(i)}d^{-2\sum_{k=2}^{n}i_{k}}\right)\cdot\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle\right)\Bigm|_{\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}}.

Step 2

Next, we consider the homology classes given by the support of
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}
and
\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\bar{N}}
where we remove the arc and circle that end or go around the last puncture, as in Figure
6.5
:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}\in H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}}.

\hskip 14.22636pt\downarrow\text{ lifts }

Figure 6.5
:
Removing the middle circle and the extremal circle from the geometric support

In this step we want to compare the graded intersection from Step I, given by

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle

and the new pairing between the classes from Figure
6.5
, given by:

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle.

The unique difference between the intersections of the underlying pairs of submanifolds comes from the fact that the first pair of submanifolds has an extra intersection point between the extremal circle and the arc that ends in the puncture
2n
, as in Figure
6.6
:

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ and }\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle

Figure 6.6
:
Removing the middle circle and the extremal circle for the coloured Alexander polynomials

That means that the associated intersection pairing has an extra coefficient coming from the contribution of this intersection point.

This is a computation which is very similar to the analog one presented in
Step 2
for the case of the Coloured Jones polynomials (which we saw in the previous section).

This coefficient comes from the monodromy of the loop around the
s
-puncture, presented in the left hand side of Figure
6.6
, which gets evaluated to the variable
y
.

Then, we have the following relation between the two intersection pairings:

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle=y\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle

Then, let us specialise these intersections via the change of variables
\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}
. We recall that:

\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}(y)=d(\lambda_{C(1)})=[\lambda_{C(1)}]_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}.

So, we obtain the following relation:

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}}=[\lambda_{C(1)}]_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}\cdot\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}}

Step 3

Now we turn our attention to the definition of the coloured Alexander invariants, coming from representation theory. We will use the version of the quantum group
U_{q}(sl(2))
over two variables, which has the divided powers of one of the generators. We refer to
[
3
, Section 3]
for the definition of this quantum group, its Verma modules and representation theory.

We showed in
[
3
, Section 3]
that we can specialise this representation theory at roots of unity and obtain the coloured Alexander invariant for a link whose components are coloured with the same colour.
We did this by first considering the subspace generated by the first
\mathcal{N}
weight vectors in the Verma module. Then, we noticed that when we specialise at the root of unity
\xi_{\mathcal{N}}
, this subspace does not lead to a well-defined sub-representation over the quantum group
U_{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}(sl(2))
. However, the key point is that the tensor product of this specialised module is preserved by the specialised braid group action.

This was shown in
[
3
, Lemma 3.1.7]
, and it relies on the computation of the R-matrix action onto a tensor product of two vectors
v_{i}\otimes v_{j}
from the Verma module together with the fact that when specialised at roots of unity we have a vanishing of certain quantum factorials. That ensures that if
i,j\leqslant\mathcal{N}-1
, then the action of the specialised braiding on
v_{i}\otimes v_{j}
will be a linear combination of
v_{i-n}\otimes v_{j+n}
where all the indices remain bounded by
\mathcal{N}
.

For our situation, namely for the coloured Alexander invariants for links, we need to work with different colours. This means that we have to consider tensor products of modules associated to different highest weights
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
.

In a nutshell, the main property that changes and needs to be checked concerns the action of the braiding on such two Verma modules that have weights
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
.

We notice that also for this situation we can generalise the argument from
[
3
, Lemma 3.1.7]
. A direct computation of the coefficients coming from the R-matrix shows that we have the property that the tensor power of finite dimensional subspaces of dimension
\mathcal{N}
associated to Verma modules of different weights are preserved by the braid group action,
when specialised to the root of unity
\xi_{\mathcal{N}}
.

This means that we can obtain the coloured Alexander polynomial of the link coloured with different colours via the Reshetikhin-Turaev construction applied to this version of the quantum group. More precisely, looking on the bottom of the diagram, we have that the cups evaluated on the algebraic side correspond exactly to the sum classes
\mathscr{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}
over all choices of indices
\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},...,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}
.
After that, we use the property that the braid action on the quantum side and on the homological side are isomorphic, via the identification showed by Martel in
[
20
]
.

Step 4

The upper part of the diagram, given by the set of caps is associated on the algebraic side to the evaluations. This means that we evaluate non-trivially just the tensor powers of vectors from the specialised Verma module which satisfy the following two requirements:

•

components are all strictly less than
\mathcal{N}

•

the indices of the components are symmetric with respect to the colour
\mathcal{N}
, meaning that they have the sum
\mathcal{N}-1
(if we remove the first component, because this is associated to the open strand).

Now, we translate the meaning of these evaluation conditions on the homological side.

We notice that this condition gives us the requirement that we have to evaluate non-trivially just the homology classes which:

•

have all multiplicities of the multi-indices strictly less than
\mathcal{N}

•

the number of arcs that end in symmetric punctures with respect to
0
(which is in the middle of the punctured disc) adds up to
\mathcal{N}-1
, excluding the components associated to the puncture labelled by
1
(they are associated to the open strand).

Step 5

Looking more closely at this requirement, we see that it enforces that we evaluate non-trivially just the classes whose indices associated to the p-punctures
k
and
2n+1-k
have the sum
\mathcal{N}-1
. The main point is that we are able to obtain this condition in a geometric way, by intersecting with the dual class
\mathscr{L}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}
.

Step 6

Algebraically, we have to encode an additional piece of data, which is a coefficient corresponding to the caps of the diagram. On the representation theory side, this is provided by the quantum trace. The details of the argument for the case where we have just a single colour are discussed in
[
3
, (Section 5, Section 8)]
. We will proceed with an analog argument, so we present below the main differences that appears in this new situation, given by the choice of different colours for our link.

The quantum trace is a trace that is twisted by certain coefficients that come from the pivotal structure. In this case, the pivotal structure is provided by the action of the element
K^{\mathcal{N}-1}
of the quantum group (in
[
3
]
we present in details the precise definition of this quantum trace).

Now, we fix a set of indices
i_{1},...,i_{n-1}
which are strictly bounded by
\mathcal{N}
. On the algebraic side, the action of the element
K^{\mathcal{N}-1}
on the associated monomial has the following coefficient:

(6.11)

{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}^{(\mathcal{N}-1)\sum_{k=2}^{n}\left((\lambda_{C(k)})-2i_{k-1}\right)}=\left(\prod_{k=2}^{n}{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}^{(\mathcal{N}-1)\lambda_{C(k)}}\right)\cdot{\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}^{\ \sum_{k=1}^{n-1}2i_{k}}.

In the following part, we encode this coefficient using the specialisation of variables which are used for the local system.

We recall the following property:

(6.12)

\displaystyle\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}(u_{C(i)})={\xi_{\mathcal{N}}}^{(1-\mathcal{N})\lambda_{C(i)}},\forall i\in\{1,...,n\}\ (\text{following }\eqref{eq:N})

\displaystyle\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}(d^{-2})=\xi_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}.

Using this, we obtain that the coefficient from (
6.11
) is precisely the following specialisation:

(6.13)

\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{N}}\left(\left(\prod_{i=2}^{n}u^{-1}_{C(i)}\right)\cdot d^{-2\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}i_{k}}\right).

It remains one last coefficient in the formula presented in (
6.8
). This comes from the framing contribution of the components of the link
L
.

Putting all of these together, we conclude the intersection model for the coloured Alexander polynomial of the link
L
coloured with colours
\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{l}
.
∎

7
Unifying all Coloured Jones polynomials with colours bounded by
\mathcal{N}

In this part we put together the provious models for the coloured Jones polynomials for knots and we will show that if we fix
\mathcal{N}
, then the intersection at level
\mathcal{N}
recovers all coloured Jones polynomials of levels less than
\mathcal{N}
, as presented in Theorem
2.9
which we remind below.

Theorem 7.1

(Unifying coloured Jones polynomials of bounded level)

Let us fix a level
\mathcal{N}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the state sum of Lagrangian intersection in the
configuration space of
(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)+2
points in the punctured disc:

(7.1)

\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}[u^{\pm 1},x^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]

(given by the set of Lagrangian intersections
\{\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\rangle\}_{\bar{i}=(i_{1},...,i_{n})\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}}
)

recovers all coloured Jones polynomials of colours
\mathcal{M}\leqslant\mathcal{N}
, as below:

(7.2)

\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{M}}(K)

\displaystyle=~\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\Bigm|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}}.

Proof.

Let us start by recalling the definition of the intersection form at level
\mathcal{N}
:

(7.3)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})=u^{f}\cdot u^{-(n-1)}\cdot\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle.

Similarly, the intersection form at the inferior level,
\mathcal{M}
, has the formula:

(7.4)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta_{n})=u^{f}\cdot u^{-(n-1)}\cdot\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{M}-1}\}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\right\rangle.

Following the topological model presented in Theorem
2.6
and Theorem
2.8
, we have that
\Gamma^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta_{n})
and
J{\Gamma}^{\bar{N}}(\beta_{n})
recover the
\mathcal{M}^{th}
coloured Alexander and coloured Jones polynomials, through the specialisation of coefficients
\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\psi_{\mathcal{M}}
, respectively as below:

(7.5)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\bar{\lambda}}(K)

\displaystyle=~\left(\Gamma^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta_{n})\right)\Bigm|_{\psi^{\bar{\lambda}}_{\mathcal{M}}}

\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{M}}(K)

\displaystyle=~\left(J\Gamma^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta_{n})\right)\Bigm|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}}.

We remark that for the case of knots
K=\hat{\beta_{n}}
, the two associated intersections actually coincide, and so we have:

(7.6)

\Gamma^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta_{n})=J\Gamma^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta_{n}),\forall\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{N}.

Putting together these equations, we see that it is enough to prove that the state sums of Lagrangian intersections
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})
and
\Gamma^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta_{n})
become equal when specialised through
\psi_{\mathcal{M}}
:

(7.7)

\displaystyle\left(\Gamma^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta_{n})\right)\Bigm|_{\psi^{J}_{\mathcal{M}}}=~\left(\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\right)\Bigm|_{\psi^{J}_{\mathcal{M}}}

for all levels
\mathcal{M}
bounded by
\mathcal{N}
.

Let us prove relation (
7.7
), which will show that we recover all coloured Jones invariants of level bounded by
\mathcal{N}
. Using the formulas from equation (
7.3
) and (
7.4
), we want to prove the following relation:

(7.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{M}-1}\}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi_{\mathcal{N}}}.

Looking at the second sum we notice that we have two types of terms, namely the ones associated to indices less than
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
and the ones which are in between
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}
, so we want to show the following relation:

(7.9)

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{M}-1}\}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{M}-1}\}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}}+

\displaystyle+\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{\mathcal{M}},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}}.

We will do this in two parts. First we show that the terms from the left and right hand side of the above equation that are associated to an index
\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{M}-1}\}
become equal when specialised at level
\mathcal{M}
. More specifically we will prove the following relation:

(7.10)

\displaystyle\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}}=\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}},

\displaystyle\forall\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{M}-1}\}.

Secondly, the terms associated to higher indices
\bar{i}\in\{\bar{\mathcal{M}},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}
vanish when specialised at level
\mathcal{M}
. More specifically we will prove the following relation:

(7.11)

\displaystyle\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}}=0,\ \forall\bar{i}\in\{\overline{\mathcal{M}},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}.

Step I - Classes that are associated to a multi-index bounded by
\mathcal{M}

In order to prove the first part, namely relation (
7.10
), we will actually show a slightly stronger property, namely that the classes corresponding to the indices
(\bar{i},\mathcal{M})
and
(\bar{i},\mathcal{N})
give the same intersection as long as the index is bounded by
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
, as below.

Lemma 7.2

(Equality of intersections associated to indices less than
\mathcal{M}
)

The following intersection pairings, between classes associated to indices
\bar{i}
bounded by
\bar{\mathcal{M}}
give the same result, even before specialisation:

(7.12)

\displaystyle\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\right\rangle=\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle,\forall\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{M}-1}\}.

Proof.

Looking at the classes
\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}
we remark that they have the same geometric support in the left hand side of the puncture disc. The only difference is that the class
\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}
comes from a support that has more segments ending in the right hand side of the disc.

Now, we have to act with the braid
\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+1}
. This action does not change the support associated to the right hand side of the disc.

So, we remark that the classes
\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+1}\rangle)\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}
and
\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+1}\rangle)\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}
have the same geometric support in the left hand side of the disc, the only difference coming from the number of arcs ending in the right hand side of the disc.

Now, we have to intersect with the dual classes, which are
\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}
. Looking at the geometric supports that give the submanifolds in the base configuration space, we remark that we have a bijective correspondence between the associated sets of intersection points, as below:

(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}F_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}L_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\longleftrightarrow^{f}(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}F_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}L_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}

\bar{x}\ \ \ \longleftrightarrow\ \ \ f(\bar{x}).

In order to construct this bijection, let us consider an intersection point
\bar{x}\in(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}F_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}L_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}
. We define
f(\bar{x})
to be the point in the configuration space coming from
\bar{x}
where we add
(\mathcal{N}-M)(n-1)
particles. More specifically, we add to
\bar{x}
the set of points situated in the right hand side of the disc, at the intersection between the extra arcs ending in a puncture labeled by
\{n+1,...,2n-1\}
(from the support of
(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}F_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}
) and the circles that give
L_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}
.

In order to compute the intersections we use the formula from (
4.17
). Having in mind that our submanifolds come from products of arcs, we can compute the intersections by looking at the product of signs at the local orientations in the punctured disc, associated to
\bar{x}
and
f(\bar{x})
.

(7.13)

\displaystyle\ll(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\gg

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{x}\in(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}F_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}L_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}}\alpha_{\bar{x}}\cdot\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{M}}}(l_{\bar{x}})

\displaystyle\ll(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\gg

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{y}\in(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}F_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}}\alpha_{\bar{y}}\cdot\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}(l_{\bar{y}})=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{x}\in(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}F_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}L_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}}\alpha_{f(\bar{x})}\cdot\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}(l_{f(\bar{x}})).

Now, looking at the components of the intersection points
\bar{x}
and
f(\bar{x})
, we see that the points in the left hand side of the disc coincide, so they have the same local signs and all the components from the right hand side have positive orientation, so they do not contribute to the sign cunt. This shows that:

(7.14)

\alpha_{\bar{x}}=\alpha_{f(\bar{x})}.

Next, let us look at the monomials that are associated to these intersection points. The loop in the configuration space which is associated to
f(\bar{x})
,
l_{f(\bar{x})}
, can be obtained from the loop associated to
\bar{x}
union with another set of loops that pass through the extra intersection points in the right hand side of the disc (which we used in order to construct the bijection
f
).

However, we remark that these extra loops are evaluated trivially by the local system
\bar{\phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
. This is due to the fact that they do not twist and do not go around any puncture, and so they contribute with coefficients which are all 1.

The last remark is that the two local systems
\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{M}}}
and
\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}
evaluate in the same way the loops associated to the punctures in the left hand side of the disc, and also the relative winding. So, the contribution of the components of the intersection points from the left hand side of the disc is the same, when evaluated through these two local systems.

Putting everything together, we conclude that:

(7.15)

\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{M}}}(\bar{x})=\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}(f(\bar{x})),\ \ \ \ \forall\bar{x}\in(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}F_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}L_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}.

This shows that the two intersection pairings lead to the same result:

(7.16)

\ll(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\gg=\ll(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\gg.

In order to get to the pairings that we are interested in, we still have to change the coefficients via the colouring
f_{C}
, following relation (
4.19
). Specialising the previous relation we get:

(7.17)

\displaystyle\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\rangle=

\displaystyle=\ll(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\gg|_{f_{C}}=

\displaystyle=\ll(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\gg|_{f_{C}}=

\displaystyle=\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\rangle.

∎

Step II- Classes associated to higher indices, specialised at level
\mathcal{M}

Now, we will investigate what happens with the classes associated to multi-indices bigger than
\mathcal{M}
, when specialised via the change of coefficients at level
\mathcal{M}
. We will prove that in this situation the specialisation vanishes, which is what we wanted in relation (
7.11
).

Lemma 7.3

(Vanishing of the intersection pairing at inferior levels)

(7.18)

\displaystyle\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}}=0,\forall\bar{i}\in\{\overline{\mathcal{M}},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}.

Proof.

We will prove this relation in a topological way, showing that something special happens with the geometry of the above class when we specialise it to a root of unity or at natural parameters, if we are in the case of knot closures.

First let us look at the action
(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}
.

We will use the structure of the homology group
H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}
. Following
[
9
]
, this homology group has a basis that is generated by the following set of elements:

(7.19)

\mathscr{B}=\{\mathscr{U}^{\prime}_{j_{0},...,j_{2n+1}}\mid j_{0},...,j_{2n+1}\in\mathbb{N},j_{0}+...+j_{2n+1}=(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)+2\}

where
\mathscr{U}^{\prime}_{j_{0},...,j_{2n+1}}
is the homology class given by the geometric support from Figure
7.1
:

Figure 7.1
:
Basis for homology

This means that after we act with the braid, there exists a collection of coefficients
\alpha_{\bar{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]
such that:

(7.20)

(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n}){\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{j}=(j_{0},...,j_{2n+1})\\
j_{0}+...+j_{2n+1}=(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)+2\end{subarray}}\alpha_{\bar{j}}\cdot\mathscr{U}^{\prime}_{\bar{j}}.

We remark that the action modifies the geometric support of the class just around the punctures from the left hand side of the disc, since on the right hand side we have the trivial braid. This means that

(7.21)

(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n}){\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{j}=(j_{0},...,j_{n-1})\end{subarray}}\alpha_{\bar{j}}\cdot\mathscr{F}_{\tilde{j},\tilde{i}}.

where we denote

F_{\tilde{j},\tilde{i}}:=\mathscr{U}^{\prime}_{\tilde{j},\tilde{i}}

for
\tilde{i}=(1,\mathcal{N}-1-j_{n-1},...,\mathcal{N}-1-j_{1},1,0).

Next, we look at the intersection with the dual class. We have the following property of the intersection form:

(7.22)

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle=0\text{ if }(j_{0},j_{1},...,j_{n})\neq(0,i_{1},...,i_{n-1}).

This comes from a similar argument as the one presented in Step
4
and Step
5
from the proof of Theorem
6.7
.
So our intersection form sees just the coefficient associated to the index
(0,i_{1},...,i_{n})
:
\alpha_{\bar{i}}
from formula (
7.21
).

For the next part, we follow a parallel phenomenon which happens on the algebraic side. The idea is that thanks to the fact that we work with knots, the induced braid action on the set of punctures from the left hand side of the disc permutes all these punctures between themselves by a cyclic permutation of maximum length:

\sigma
(its order is
n
).

We look at the support of the homology class
\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}
and we start with the first puncture whose associated index is
i_{0}=0
. After we act with the braid, this will contribute to the coefficients associated to the puncture
\sigma(1)
. Let us see what happens if
\sigma(1)\geqslant\mathcal{M}
. This means that through our braid action we increase this index to jump over
\mathcal{M}
at some point. Now, we use the form of the braid action: the homological braid action corresponds to an
R
-matrix that uses powers of generators
E
and
F^{(n)}
, via the identification between quantum and homological representations from
[
20
]
, Lemma 6.37. Roughly speaking
E
always decreases the index and the generators
F^{(n)}
when acted on a component
v_{i}
with index
i
contribute to the coefficient by the formula:

(7.23)

F^{(n)}v_{i}\simeq{n+i\brack i}_{d}\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(xd^{k}-1)v_{i+n}.

Here
\simeq
means up to an invertible factor.

This means that if we arrive through our braid action in the puncture
\sigma(1)
at an index such that
i_{\sigma(1)}>\mathcal{M}
, since we started from
0
at some point we acted with

(7.24)

F^{(n)}v_{i}\simeq{n+i\brack i}_{d}\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(xd^{k}-1)v_{i+n}

for
i<\mathcal{M}
and
i+n\geqslant\mathcal{M}
.

Remark 7.4

(Specialisations associated to coloured Jones polynomials give vanishing coefficients)

(7.25)

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathcal{M}}\left({n+i\brack i}_{d}\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(xd^{k}-1)\right)=0

for
i<\mathcal{M}
and
i+n\geqslant\mathcal{M}
.
This means that if
i_{\sigma(1)}>\mathcal{M}
we have:
\alpha_{\bar{i}}=0
. Inductively, since
\sigma
is a cycle, if we have a component
i_{k}>\mathcal{M}
then
\alpha_{\bar{i}}=0
, so our intersection sees just the classes with multi-indixes
\bar{i}
that have all components less than
\mathcal{M}
, when specialised through the specialisations at level
\mathcal{M}
. This concludes the proof of the annulation of the specialised intersection form.

∎

Now, we put together these properties in order to finish the proof of the globalisation property. Following Lemma
7.2
and
7.3
, we have:

(7.26)

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{M}-1}\}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}}+

\displaystyle+\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{\mathcal{M}},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}}=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{M}-1}\}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{M}}}\right\rangle|_{\psi_{\mathcal{M}}}.

This shows the relation that we wanted, presented in equation (
7.9
) and so relation (
7.7
) holds. This concludes the proof that our intersection pairing recovers all coloured Jones polynomials for levels bounded by
\mathcal{M}
, as stated in Theorem
7.1
.

∎

7.1
Graded intersection in the ring with integer coefficients

Lemma 7.5

For any braid (in general, not just for braids that give knot closures), the graded intersections
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})
and
J{\Gamma}^{\bar{N}}(\beta_{n})
take values in the Laurent polynomial ring with integer coefficients:

\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n}),J{\Gamma}^{\bar{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},...,u_{l}^{\pm 1},x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}].

Proof.

We remind the formula for this intersection form:

(7.27)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})

\displaystyle:=\prod_{i=1}^{l}u_{i}^{\left(f_{i}-\sum_{j\neq{i}}lk_{i,j}\right)}\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n}u^{-1}_{C(i)}\cdot

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{i}\in\{\bar{0},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{N}-1}\}}\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle.

Since the homology classes
\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}
belong to the homology groups
H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{n,2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}
and
H^{\bar{\mathcal{N}},\partial}_{n,2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}
that are modules over
\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]
, we have that a priori

\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{C}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},...,u_{l}^{\pm 1},x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}].

We will show that for our particular homology classes, we have the property that actually their intersection pairing has all coefficients that are integers:

(7.28)

\left\langle(\beta_{n}\cup{\mathbb{I}}_{n+2})\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathscr{F}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0}\mathscr{L}_{\bar{i},\mathcal{N}}}\right\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}].

As we have seen in Proposition
4.37
, we have

\left\langle~,~\right\rangle=\ \ll~,~\gg|_{f_{C}}

and the formula for
\left\langle~,~\right\rangle
is the same as the one from Proposition
4.24
, specialised via
f_{C}
.

We will use Remark
4.25
, and then specialise the coefficients through
f_{C}
.
For our homology classes, their intersection will be paramatrised by the intersection points between their associated submanifolds in the base configuration space, graded by
\bar{\psi}^{\mathcal{N}}
.
So it is enough to show that
\tilde{\psi}^{\mathcal{N}}
evaluated on the loops that are associated to our intersection points takes integer coefficients.
Following relation (
4.15
) we remind that

\bar{\psi}^{\mathcal{N}}=\eta\circ\tilde{\psi}^{\mathcal{N}}.

The monodromies of the local system
\tilde{\psi}^{\mathcal{N}}
are:

•

x_{i}^{2}
around the
p
-punctures from the left hand side of the disc, (
i\in\{1,...,n\}
)

•

x_{i}^{2}\cdot(d^{\prime})^{2(\mathcal{N}_{i}-1)}
around the
p
-punctures punctures from the right hand side of the disc (
i\in\{n+1,...,2n\}
)

•

y_{1},...,y_{l}
around the
s
-punctures

•

(d^{\prime})^{2}
for the relative twisting.

So
\tilde{\psi}^{\mathcal{N}}
evaluates classes of loops with even powers of
d^{\prime}
. This means that when composing with the change of coefficients
\eta
, given by:

(7.29)

\displaystyle\eta:\mathbb{Z}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\prime\pm 1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{n}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\eta(x_{i})=x_{i}\\
\eta(y)=y\\
\eta(d^{\prime})=id.\end{cases}

as in (
4.14
), we obtain integer coefficients.

This shows that (
7.28
) holds and so our intersection form has integer coefficients:

\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},...,u_{l}^{\pm 1},x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}].

The same argument applies also for the intersection form:

J{\Gamma}^{\bar{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}[u_{1}^{\pm 1},...,u_{l}^{\pm 1},x_{1}^{\pm 1},...,x_{l}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}].

∎

8
\mathcal{N}^{th}
Unified Jones invariant

In this section our aim is to construct a knot invariant
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)
starting from the graded intersection in the confogiration space
\mathrm{Conf}_{2+(n-1)(\mathcal{N}-1)}\left(\mathbb{D}_{2n+2}\right)
:

\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n}).

This invariant is defined in a fixed configuration space will unify all coloured Jones polynomials up to level
\mathcal{N}
.

The first part for this construction concerns the definition the appropiate ring of coefficients where these invariants will be defined. Secondly, we will show that in such a universal ring, we have indeed a well defined knot invariant.

8.1
Set-up and notations

Definition 8.1

(Rings for the universal invariant)

Let us define the following rings:

(8.1)

\begin{cases}\mathbb{L}:=\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\\
\Lambda^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}=\mathbb{Z}[d^{\pm 1}].\end{cases}

From now on, since we work with knots, we will set
y=1
and
u=x
, as explained in Convention
2.10
. Then our intersection
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})
recovers the normalised versions of the
\mathcal{N}^{th}
coloured Jones and Alexander polynomials. We define
J_{\mathcal{N}}
to be the
{\mathcal{N}}^{th}
normalised coloured Jones polynomial and we will look at it in the variable
d
(which corresponds to
q^{-1}
from the previous sections).

So we have our intersection

\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}].

Definition 8.2

(Specialisation for the universal invariant)

We change the ring of coefficients as above, and let us define the associated globalised specialisation of coefficients as below:

\psi^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}:\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{Z}[x^{\pm 1},d^{\pm 1}]\rightarrow\Lambda^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}

(8.2)

\psi^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}(x)=d^{1-\mathcal{N}}

8.2
Product up to a finite level

Definition 8.3

(Product up to level
\mathcal{N}
)

For a fixed level
\mathcal{N}
, let us define the product of the rings for smaller levels, as:

(8.3)

\displaystyle\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{N}}^{J}:=\prod_{\mathcal{M}\leqslant\mathcal{N}}\Lambda_{\mathcal{M}}^{J}.

and
p^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{M}}:\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{N}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{\mathcal{M}}^{J}
the projection onto the
\mathcal{M}^{th}
component.

Also, let us denote by
\tilde{\psi}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}:\mathbb{L}\rightarrow\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{N}}^{J}
the product of specialisations:

(8.4)

\tilde{\psi}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}:=\prod_{\mathcal{M}\leqslant\mathcal{N}}\psi^{J}_{\mathcal{M}}

Definition 8.4

(Coloured invariants up to a fixed level)

We denote the product of the invariants up to level
\mathcal{N}
as below:

(8.5)

\displaystyle\hat{J}^{\mathcal{N}}(K):=\prod_{\mathcal{M}\leqslant\mathcal{N}}J_{\mathcal{M}}(K)\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{N}}^{J}.

We will construct our universal ring using this sequence of morphisms
\{\tilde{\psi}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\mid\mathcal{N}\in\mathbb{N}\}
. More specifically, we will use the sequence of kernels associated to these maps, as follows.

Definition 8.5

(Kernels and quotient rings)

Let us denote by:

(8.6)

\displaystyle\tilde{I}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}:=\text{Ker}\left(\tilde{\psi}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\right)\subseteq\mathbb{L}.

We also denote the quotient rings associated to these ideals by:

(8.7)

\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}:=\mathbb{L}/\tilde{I}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}.

We remind that we have the product of the rings for smaller levels:

(8.8)

\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{N}}^{J}:=\prod_{\mathcal{M}\leqslant\mathcal{N}}\Lambda_{\mathcal{M}}^{J}.

Definition 8.6

(Induced maps on quotient rings)

This means that we have the following diagram with quotient maps, which we denote
\bar{\bar{\psi}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
and
g^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
, as below:

\mathbb{L}
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in
\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{N}}^{J}
\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}
\Lambda^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)}\in
g^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
\tilde{\psi}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
\bar{\bar{\psi}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
\bar{\psi}^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{N}}
p^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{N}}
Def.
8.10

\psi^{u,J}_{\mathcal{N}}

Figure 8.1
:
Quotients of the intersection form

We also define the projection map:

(8.9)

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{N}}:\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{\mathcal{M}}^{J}

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{M}}:=p^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{M}}\circ\bar{\bar{\psi}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}.

Remark 8.7

By the definition of the product rings
\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{N}}^{J}
, we see that we have a sequence of nested ideals:

(8.10)

\displaystyle\cdots\supseteq\tilde{I}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\supseteq\tilde{I}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}+1}\supseteq\cdots

Definition 8.8

(Sequence of quotient rings)

In this manner, we obtain a sequence of associated quotient rings, with maps between them:

(8.11)

\displaystyle l^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\hskip 28.45274ptl^{J}_{\mathcal{N}+1}

\displaystyle\cdots\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\leftarrow\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}+1}\leftarrow\cdots

Definition 8.9

(Limit ring)

We define the projective limit of this sequence of rings and denote it as follows:

(8.12)

\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}:=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}.

Definition 8.10

Then, we have induced maps:

(8.13)

\bar{\psi}^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{N}}:\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}\rightarrow\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{N}}^{J}.

Further, composing with the projection onto the
\mathcal{N}^{th}
component we obtain the following map:

(8.14)

\psi^{u,J}_{\mathcal{N}}:\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}\rightarrow\Lambda^{J}_{\mathcal{N}},

defined as

\psi^{u,J}_{\mathcal{N}}:=p^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{N}}\circ\bar{\psi}^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{N}}.

Definition 8.11

(Projection maps for the coloured Jones coefficient rings)

We have projection maps which go from the coefficient rings at consecutive levels, as follows:

(8.15)

\text{pr}_{\mathcal{N}}^{J}:\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{N}+1}^{J}\rightarrow\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{N}}^{J}.

Now we are going to define step by step the universal invariant, coming from the geometric data given by our intersection pairing.

8.3
\mathcal{N}^{th}
Unified Jones invariant

First, we define a set of knot invariants that will be used to build the globalised coloured Jones invariant. We recall that we have the graded intersection:

\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\in\mathbb{L}

and it recovers the coloured Jones polynomials, as below:

(8.16)

\psi^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\left(\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\right)=J_{\mathcal{N}}(K).

Taking the product component-wise, we conclude that:

(8.17)

\tilde{\psi}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\left(\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\right)=\hat{J}^{\mathcal{N}}(K).

Definition 8.12

(Level
\mathcal{N}
Jones-quotient)

Let us consider the intersection form obtained from
\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})
by taking the quotient through the projection
g^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
(as in Figure
8.1
):

(8.18)

\displaystyle\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N}}(K)=g_{\mathcal{N}}(\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})).

Now we will prove Theorem
2.11
which we remind below.

Theorem 8.13

(
\mathcal{N}^{th}
unified Jones invariant)

The intersection
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)
is a well-defined knot invariant unifying all coloured Jones polynomials up to level
\mathcal{N}
:

\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)|_{\hat{\psi}^{\mathcal{N},J}_{\mathcal{M}}}=J_{\mathcal{M}}(K),\forall\mathcal{M}\leqslant\mathcal{N}.

Proof.

On one hand we have we have:

\tilde{\psi}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}\left(\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})\right)=\hat{J}^{\mathcal{N}}(K)

for
L=\hat{\beta_{n}}
. On the other hand, following Definition
8.12
, the projection
g^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
is defined from the map
\tilde{\psi}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}
and we have:

(8.19)

\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N}}(K)=g_{\mathcal{N}}(\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}(\beta_{n})).

Putting these properties together, it follows that

\bar{\bar{\psi}}_{\mathcal{N}}\left(\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N}}(K)\right)=\hat{J}^{\mathcal{N}}(K).

On the other hand, from Definition
8.6
of the quotient rings, we know that
\bar{\bar{\psi}}_{\mathcal{N}}
is injective. Also,
\hat{J}^{\mathcal{N}}(K)
is given by the product of all coloured Jones invariants up to level
\mathcal{N}
, so it is a knot invariant.

So, we conclude that
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N}}(K)
is mapped to the knot invariant
\hat{J}^{\mathcal{N}}(K)
, through the injective function
\bar{\bar{\psi}}_{\mathcal{N}}
. This means that
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N}}(K)
is a well-defined knot invariant.

This knot invariant recovers all coloured Jones polynomials up to level
\mathcal{N}
, using the definition of the quotient morphisms from (
8.9
). This concludes the proof of the structure of this knot invariant.

∎

9
Universal Coloured Jones Invariant

In this part our aim is to unify and show that we can define an invariant out of the sequence:

\{\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N}}(K)\mid\mathcal{N}\in\mathbb{N}\}.

9.1
Definition of the universal invariant

First we recall the Definition
8.9
which contains the formula for the appropiate ring of coefficients where this universal invariant will be defined

\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}:=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}_{\mathcal{N}}.

Next we show that in such a universal ring, we have indeed a well defined knot invariant which is a universal coloured Jones invariant, recovering all
J_{\mathcal{N}}(K)
for all colours
\mathcal{N}\in\mathbb{N}
.
This will be constructed from the sequence of intersections up to level
\mathcal{N}
.

Definition 9.1

(Unification of all coloured Jones knot invariants)

Let us consider the projective limit of the graded intersection pairings
\hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)
over all levels, and denote it as follows:

(9.1)

{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K):=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\mathrm{lim}}\ \hat{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{N},J}(K)\in\hat{\mathbb{L}}^{J}.

Definition 9.2

(Limit ring of coefficients and limit invariant)

Now, let us consider the product of the rings where the coloured Jones invariants belong to, where we put no condition about the level:

(9.2)

\hat{\Lambda}^{J}:=\prod_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathbb{N}}\Lambda_{\mathcal{M}}^{J}

(9.3)

\hat{J}(K):=\prod_{\mathcal{M}\leqslant\mathcal{N}}J_{\mathcal{M}}(K)\in\hat{\Lambda}^{J}.

Using the definition from above, let us denote the projection map:

(9.4)

\hat{p}_{\mathcal{M}}:\hat{\Lambda}^{J}\rightarrow\Lambda_{\mathcal{M}}^{J}.

We remark that this means that projecting onto the
\mathcal{M}^{th}
component we obtain the
\mathcal{M}^{th}
coloured Jones invariant:

(9.5)

\hat{p}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{J}(K))=J_{\mathcal{M}}(K).

Putting together all these tools now we are ready to show Theorem
1.2
, which we recall as follows.

Theorem 9.3

(Universal coloured Jones Invariant)

The limit of the invariants via the graded intersections
{\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)
is a knot invariant recovering all coloured Jones polynomials:

(9.6)

J_{\mathcal{N}}(K)={\large\hat{{\Huge{\Gamma}}}^{J}}(K)|_{\psi^{u,J}_{\mathcal{N}}}.
